<!-- page 3 -->
Foreword

The National Curriculum Framework, 2005, recommends that children’s life at school
must be linked to their life outside the school. This principle marks a departure from
the legacy of bookish learning which continues to shape our system and causes a gap
between the school, home and community. The syllabi and textbooks developed on
the basis of NCF signify an attempt to implement this basic idea. They also attempt to
discourage rote learning and the maintenance of sharp boundaries between different
subject areas. We hope these measures will take us significantly further in the direction
of a child-centred system of education outlined in the National Policy on Education
(1986).

The success of this effort depends on the steps that school principals and teachers
will take to encourage children to reflect on their own learning and to pursue imaginative
activities and questions. We must recognise that, given space, time and freedom,
children generate new knowledge by engaging with the information passed on to them
by adults. Treating the prescribed textbook as the sole basis of examination is one of
the key reasons why other resources and sites of learning are ignored. Inculcating
creativity and initiative is possible if we perceive and treat children as participants in
learning, not as receivers of a fixed body of knowledge.

These aims imply considerable change in school routines and mode of functioning.
Flexibility in the daily time-table is as necessary as rigour in implementing the annual
calendar so that the required number of teaching days are actually devoted to teaching.
The methods used for teaching and evaluation will also determine how effective this
textbook proves for making children’s life at school a happy experience, rather than a
source of stress or boredom. Syllabus designers have tried to address the problem of
curricular burden by restructuring and reorienting knowledge at different stages with
greater consideration for child psychology and the time available for teaching. The
textbook attempts to enhance this endeavour by giving higher priority and space to
opportunities for contemplation and wondering, discussion in small groups, and
activities requiring hands-on experience.

<!-- page 5 -->
Preface

The National Council of Educational esearch and Training (NCET) had constituted
21 Focus roups on Teaching of various subjects related to School Education, to
review the National Curriculum Framework for School Education - 2000 (NCFSE -
2000) in face of new emerging challenges and transformations occurring in the fields
of content and pedagogy under the contexts of National and International spectrum of
school education. These Focus roups made general and specific comments in their
respective areas. Consequently, based on these reports of Focus roups, National
Curriculum Framework (NCF)-2005 was developed.

NCET designed the new syllabi and constituted Textbook evelopment Teams
for Classes I and II to prepare textbooks in mathematics under the new guidelines
and new syllabi. The textbook for Class I is already in use, which was brought in
2005.

The first draft of the present book (Class II) was prepared by the team consisting of
NCET faculty, experts and practicing teachers. The draft was refined by the
development team in different meetings. This draft of the book was exposed to a
group of practicing teachers teaching mathematics at higher secondary stage in different
parts of the country, in a review workshop organised by the NCET at elhi. The
teachers made useful comments and suggestions which were incorporated in the draft
textbook. The draft textbook was finalised by an editorial board constituted out of
the development team. Finally, the dvisory roup in Science and Mathematics and
the Monitoring Committee constituted by the Ministry, overnment of India
have approved the draft of the textbook.

In the fitness of things, let us cite some of the essential features dominating the
textbook. These characteristics have reflections in almost all the chapters. The existing
textbook contain 1 main chapters and two appendices. Each Chapter contain the
followings

▪ Introduction highlighting the importance of the topic connection with earlier
studied topics brief mention about the new concepts to be discussed in the
chapter.

▪ rganisation of chapter into sections comprising one or more conceptsub
concepts.

▪ Motivating and introducing the conceptsub concepts. Illustrations have been
provided wherever possible.

<!-- page 6 -->
$$viii$$

▪ Proofsproblem solving involving deductive or inductive reasoning, multiplicity
of approaches wherever possible have been inducted.
▪ eometric viewing visualisation of concepts have been emphasised whenever
needed.
▪ pplications of mathematical concepts have also been integrated with allied
subjects like science and social sciences.
▪ dequate and variety of examplesexercises have been given in each section.
▪ For refocusing and strengthening the understanding and skill of problem solving
and applicabilities, miscellaneous types of examplesexercises have been
provided involving two or more sub concepts at a time at the end of the chapter.
The scope of challenging problems to talented minority have been reflected
conducive to the recommendation as reflected in NCF-2005.
▪ For more motivational purpose, brief historical background of topics have been
provided at the end of the chapter and at the beginning of each chapter relevant
quotation and photograph of eminent mathematician who have contributed
significantly in the development of the topic undertaken, are also provided.
▪ astly, for direct recapitulation of main concepts, formulas and results, brief
summary of the chapter has also been provided.

Fundamental Duties

It shall be the duty of every citien of India

National Flag and the National nthem
(b) to cherish and follow the noble ideals which inspired our national struggle
for freedom
(c) to uphold and protect the sovereignty, unity and integrity of India
(d) to defend the country and render national service when called upon to
do so
(e) to promote harmony and the spirit of common brotherhood amongst all
the people of India transcending religious, linguistic and regional or
sectional diversities to renounce practices derogatory to the dignity of
women
(f) to value and preserve the rich heritage of our composite culture
(g) to protect and improve the natural environment including forests, lakes,
rivers, wildlife and to have compassion for living creatures
(h) to develop the scientific temper, humanism and the spirit of inquiry and
reform
(i) to safeguard public property and to abjure violence
(j) to strive towards excellence in all spheres of individual and collective
activity so that the nation constantly rises to higher levels of endeavour
and achievement
(k) who is a parent or guardian, to provide opportunities for education to
his child or, as the case may be, ward between the age of six and
fourteen years.

RELATIONS AND FUNCTIONS

❖ There is no permanent place in the world for ugly mathematics ... . It may
be very hard to define mathematical beauty but that is just as true of
beauty of any kind, we may not know quite what we mean by a
beautiful poem, but that does not prevent us from recognising
one when we read it. — G. H. HARDY ❖

1.1 Introduction

Recall that the notion of relations and functions, domain,
co-domain and range have been introduced in Class XI
along with different types of specific real valued functions
and their graphs. The concept of the term ‘relation’ in
mathematics has been drawn from the meaning of relation
in English language, according to which two objects or
quantities are related if there is a recognisable connection
or link between the two objects or quantities. Let A be
the set of students of Class XII of a school and B be the
set of students of Class XI of the same school. Then some
of the examples of relations from A to B are

$$x = \frac{x}{x}$$

(i) $\{(a, b) \in \mathrm{A} \times \mathrm{B}: a \text{ is brother of } b\},$

If $(a, b) \in \mathrm{R}$, we say that $a$ is related to $b$ under the relation $\mathrm{R}$ and we write as
$a \mathrm{~R} \ b$. In general, $(a, b) \in \mathrm{R}$, we do not bother whether there is a recognisable
connection or link between $a$ and $b$. As seen in Class XI, functions are special kind of
relations.

In this chapter, we will study different types of relations and functions, composition
of functions, invertible functions and binary operations.

<!-- page 15 -->
MATHEMATIC

1.2 Types of Relations

In this section, we would like to study different types of relations. e know that a
relation in a set A is a subset of A $\times$ A. Thus, the empty set $\phi$ and A $\times$ A are two
extreme relations. or illustration, consider a relation R in the set A $\{ , , \}$ given by
R $\{ ( \ a, b) : a \ \ b \ \}$. This is the empty set, as no pair (   $a, b$) satisfies the condition
$a \ \ b$ . imilarly, R       ' $\{ ( \ a, b) : a \ \ b \ \ge \}$ is the whole set A $\times$ A, as all pairs
$(a, b)$ in A $\times$ A satisfy   $a \ \ b \ \ge$ . These two extreme examples lead us to the
following definitions.

Definition 1 A relation $R$ in a set $A$ is called empty relation, if no element of $A$ is
related to any element of $A$, i.e., $R \quad \phi \sqsubset A \times A$.

Definition 2 A relation $R$ in a set $A$ is called universal relation, if each element of $A$
is related to every element of $A$, i.e., $R \quad A \times A$.

Both the empty relation and the universal relation are some times called $trivial$
$relations$.

Example 1 Let A be the set of all students of a boys school. how that the relation R
in A given by R $\{( a, b) : a \text{ is sister of } b\} \text{ is the empty relation and R}' \{( a, b) : \text{ the}
\text{difference between heights of } a \text{ and } b \text{ is less than meters}\} \text{ is the universal relation.}

Solutionince the school is boys school, no student of the school can be sister of any
student of the school. Hence, R $\quad \phi$, showing that R is the empty relation. It is also
obvious that the difference between heights of any two students of the school has to be
less than meters. This shows that R ' $A \times A$ is the universal relation.

$\mathit{Remark}$ In Class XI, we have seen two ways of representing a relation, namely raster
method and set builder method. However, a relation R in the set $\{ , , \}$ defined by R
$\{ ( \ a, \ b) : b \quad a \ \}$ is also expressed as $\quad a \ \text{R} \ b$ if and only if $b \quad a$ by many
authors. e may also use this notation, as and when convenient.

If $(a, b) \in \mathrm{R}$, we say that $a$ is related to $b$ and we denote it as $a \mathrm{~R} \ b$.

ne of the most important relation, which plays a significant role in Mathematics,
is an equivalence relation. To study equivalence relation, we first consider three
types of relations, namely reflexive, symmetric and transitive.

Definition 3 A relation $R$ in a set $A$ is called

(i) *reflexive*, if $(a, a) \in \mathrm{R}$, for every $a \in \mathrm{A}$,
(ii) *symmetric*, if $(a, a) \in \mathrm{R}$ implies that $(a, a) \in \mathrm{R}$, for all $a, a \in \mathrm{A}$.
(iii) *transitive*, if $(a, a) \in \mathrm{R}$ and $(a, a) \in \mathrm{R}$ implies that $(a, a) \in \mathrm{R}$, for all $a, a$,
$a \in \mathrm{A}$.

Definition 4 A relation R in a set A is said to be an equivalence relation if R is
reflexive, symmetric and transitive.

congruent to $T \Rightarrow T$ is congruent to $T \Rightarrow (T , T ) \in R$. Therefore, $R$ is an equivalence

1.3 Types of Functions

The notion of a function along with some special functions like identity function, constant
function, polynomial function, rational function, modulus function, signum function etc.
along with their graphs have been given in Class XI.

Addition, subtraction, multiplication and division of two functions have also been
studied. As the concept of function is of paramount importance in mathematics and
among other disciplines as well, we would like to extend our study about function from
where we finished earlier. In this section, we would like to study different types of
functions.

Consider the functions $f, f, f$ and $f$ given by the following diagrams.

$f$ are distinct, but the image of two distinct elements and of X      under $f$ is same,
namely $b$. urther, there are some elements like $e$ and $f$ in X which are not images of
any element of X under $f$, while all elements of X are images of some elements of X
under $f$. The above observations lead to the following definitions:

Definition 5 A function $f : \mathrm{X} \rightarrow$ is defined to be \textit{one-one} (or \textit{injective}), if the images
of distinct elements of $\mathrm{X}$ under $f$ are distinct, i.e., for every $x$ , $x \in \mathrm{X}, f(x)$ $f(x)$
implies $x$ $x$ . therwise, $f$ is called \textit{many-one}.

The function $f$ and $f$ in ig . (i) and (iv) are one-one and the function $f$ and $f$
in ig . (ii) and (iii) are many-one.

Definition 6 A function $f: \mathrm{X} \rightarrow$ is said to be $onto$ (or $surjective$), if every element
of is the image of some element of $\mathrm{X}$ under $f$, i.e., for every $y \in$, there exists an
element $x$ in $\mathrm{X}$ such that $f(x) \quad y$.

The function $f$ and $f$ in ig . (iii), (iv) are onto and the function $f$ in ig . (i) is
not onto as elements $e, f$ in X are not the image of any element in X under $f$ .

Definition 7 A function $f : \mathrm{X} \rightarrow$ is said to be \textit{one-one} and \textit{onto} (or \textit{bijective}), if $f$ is
both one-one and onto.

The function $f$ in ig . (iv) is one-one and onto.

$\mathit{Remark}$ The results mentioned in Examples and are also true for an arbitrary

difference between a finite and an infinite set.

1.4 Composition of Functions and Invertible Function

In this section, we will study composition of functions and the inverse of a bijective
function. Consider the set A of all students, who appeared in Class X of  a Board
Examination in . Each student appearing in the Board Examination is assigned a
roll number by the Board which is written by the students in the answer script at the
time of examination. In order to have confidentiality, the Board arranges to deface the
roll numbers of students in the answer scripts and assigns a fake code number to each
roll number. Let $\mathrm{B} \subset \mathbf{N}$ be the set of all roll numbers and $\mathrm{C} \subset \mathbf{N}$ be the set of all code
numbers. This gives rise to two functions $f : \mathrm{A} \rightarrow \mathrm{B}$ and $g : \mathrm{B} \rightarrow \mathrm{C}$ given by $f(a)$ the
roll number assigned to the student $a$ and $g(b)$ the code number assigned to the roll
number $b$. In this process each student is assigned a roll number through the function $f$
and each roll number is assigned a code number through the function $g$. Thus, by the
combination of these two functions, each student is eventually attached a code number.

This leads to the following definition:

Definition 8 Let $f : \mathrm{A} \rightarrow \mathrm{B}$ and $g : \mathrm{B} \rightarrow \mathrm{C}$ be two functions. Then the composition of
$f$ and $g$, denoted by $gof$, is defined as the function $gof : \mathrm{A} \rightarrow \mathrm{C}$ given by

$$gof(x) \quad g(f(x)), \forall x \in A.$$

Fig 1.5

1.5 Binary Operations
Right from the school days, you must have come across four fundamental operations
namely addition, subtraction, multiplication and division. The main feature of these
operations is that given any two numbers $a$ and $b$, we associate another number $a \quad b$

or $a \quad b$ or $ab$ or $\frac{a}{b}, b \neq$. It is to be noted that only two numbers can be added or
multiplied at a time. hen we need to add three numbers, we first add two numbers
and the result is then added to the third number. Thus, addition, multiplication, subtraction

<!-- page 33 -->
MATHEMATIC

and division are examples of binary operation, as ‘binary’ means two. If we want to
have a general definition which can cover all these four operations, then the set of
numbers is to be replaced by an arbitrary set X and then general binary operation is
nothing but association of any pair of elements $a, b$ from X to another element of X.
This gives rise to a general definition as follows:

Definition 10 A binary operation $*$ on a set A is a function $* \Box A \times A \rightarrow A$. e denote
$* \Box (a, b)$ by $a * b$.

Example 29 how that addition, subtraction and multiplication are binary operations
on $\mathbf{R}$, but division is not a binary operation on $\mathbf{R}$. urther, show that division is a binary
operation on the set $\mathbf{R}_*$ of nonero real numbers.

Solution : $\mathbf{R} \times \mathbf{R} \rightarrow \mathbf{R}$ is given by
$(a, b) \rightarrow a \quad b$
$: \mathbf{R} \times \mathbf{R} \rightarrow \mathbf{R}$ is given by
$(a, b) \rightarrow a \quad b$
$\times : \mathbf{R} \times \mathbf{R} \rightarrow \mathbf{R}$ is given by
$(a, b) \rightarrow a b$

ince ‘ ’ , ‘ ’ and ‘$\times$’ are functions, they are binary operations on $\mathbf{R}$.

But $\div : \mathbf{R} \times \mathbf{R} \rightarrow \mathbf{R}$, given by $(a, b) \rightarrow \frac{a}{b}$, is not a function and hence not a binary

operation, as for $b$ , $\frac{a}{b}$ is not defined.

However, $\div : \mathbf{R}_* \times \mathbf{R}_* \rightarrow \mathbf{R}_*$, given by $(a, b) \rightarrow \frac{a}{b}$ is a function and hence a
binary operation on $\mathbf{R}_*$.

Here, we are having rows and columns in the operation table with ( $i, j$) the
entry of the table being maximum of $i^{\text{th}}$ and $j^{\text{th}}$ elements of the set A. This can be
generalised for general operation $* : \text{A} \times \text{A} \rightarrow \text{A}$. If A $\{ a , a , ..., a_n \}$. Then the
operation table will be having $n$ rows and $n$ columns with $(i, j)^{\text{th}}$ entry being $a_i * a_j$.
Conversely, given any operation table having $n$ rows and $n$ columns with each entry
being an element of A $\{ a , a , ..., a_n \}$, we can define a binary operation $* : \text{A} \times \text{A} \rightarrow \text{A}$
given by $a_i * a_j$ the entry in the $i^{\text{th}}$ row and $j^{\text{th}}$ column of the operation table.

This leads to the following definition:

Definition 11 A binary operation $*$ on the set X is called commutative, if $a * b \quad b * a$,
for every $a, b \in \mathrm{X}$.

with this property of a binary operation, we can write $a * a * ... * a_n$ which is not
ambiguous. But in absence of this property, the expression $a * a * ... * a_n$ is ambiguous
unless brackets are used. Recall that in the earlier classes brackets were used whenever
subtraction or division operations or more than one operation occurred.

Example 41 If $R$ and $R$ are equivalence relations in a set $A$, show that $R \cap R$ is
also an equivalence relation.

Solutionince $\mathrm{R}$ and $\mathrm{R}$ are equivalence relations, $(a, a) \in \mathrm{R}$ , and $(a, a) \in \mathrm{R} \quad \forall a \in \mathrm{A}$.
This implies that $(a, a) \in \mathrm{R} \cap \mathrm{R}$ , $\forall a$, showing $\mathrm{R} \cap \mathrm{R}$ is reflexive. urther, ,
$(a, b) \in \mathrm{R} \cap \mathrm{R} \Rightarrow (a, b) \in \mathrm{R}$ and $(a, b) \in \mathrm{R} \Rightarrow (b, a) \in \mathrm{R}$ and $(b, a) \in \mathrm{R} \Rightarrow$
$(b, a) \in \mathrm{R} \cap \mathrm{R}$ , hence, $\mathrm{R} \cap \mathrm{R}$ is symmetric. imilarly, $(a, b) \in \mathrm{R} \cap \mathrm{R}$ and
$(b, c) \in \mathrm{R} \cap \mathrm{R} \Rightarrow (a, c) \in \mathrm{R}$ and $(a, c) \in \mathrm{R} \Rightarrow (a, c) \in \mathrm{R} \cap \mathrm{R}$ . This shows that
$\mathrm{R} \cap \mathrm{R}$ is transitive. Thus, $\mathrm{R} \cap \mathrm{R}$ is an equivalence relation.

Example 45 etermine which of the following binary operations on the set R are
associative and which are commutative.

(a) $a * b \quad \forall \ a, b \in \text{R}$ \qquad \qquad (b) $a * b \quad \frac{(a+b)}{} \quad \forall \ a, b \in \text{R}$

Solution

(a) Clearly, by definition $a * b \quad b * a \quad , \quad \forall \ a, b \in \text{R}$. Also
$(a * b) * c = ( \ * \ c)$ and $a * (b * c) \quad a * () \quad , \quad \forall \ a, b, c \in \text{R}$. Hence
R is both associative and commutative.

(b) $a * b \quad \underline{a+b} = \underline{b+a} \quad b * a$, shows that $*$ is commutative. urther, ,

$$(a * b) * c \quad \left( \underline{a+b} \right) * c.$$
$$\underline{\left( \underline{a+b} \right) + c} = \underline{a+b+ \ c}.$$

But \qquad \qquad $a * (b * c) \quad a * \left( \underline{b+c} \right)$

$$\underline{\underline{a + \underline{b+c}}} = \underline{\underline{a+b+c}} \neq \underline{\underline{a+b+ \ c}} \text{ in general.}$$

Hence, $*$ is not associative.

Example 47 Let A $\{, \}$. Then show that the number of relations containing $(, )$

**Solution** The smallest relation R containing $( , ) $ and $( , ) $ which is reflexive and

the pair $( , ) $ to R    to get R , then the relation R will be reflexive, transitive but not
symmetric. imilarly, we can obtain R    by adding $( , ) $ to R    to get the desired relation.

the total number of desired relations is three.

Summary

In this chapter, we studied different types of relations and equivalence relation,
composition of functions, invertible functions and binary operations. The main features
of this chapter are as follows:

$\blacklozenge$ Empty relation is the relation R in X given by R $\quad \phi \subset \mathrm{X} \times \mathrm{X}$.
$\blacklozenge$ Universal relation is the relation R in X given by R $\quad \mathrm{X} \times \mathrm{X}$.
$\blacklozenge$ Reflexive relation R in X is a relation with $(a, a) \in \mathrm{R} \quad \forall a \in \mathrm{X}$.
$\blacklozenge$ Symmetric relation R in X is a relation satisfying $(a, b) \in \mathrm{R}$ implies $(b, a) \in \mathrm{R}$.
$\blacklozenge$ Transitive relation R in X is a relation satisfying $(a, b) \in \mathrm{R}$ and $(b, c) \in \mathrm{R}$
implies that $(a, c) \in \mathrm{R}$.
$\blacklozenge$ Equivalence relation R in X is a relation which is reflexive, symmetric and
transitive.
$\blacklozenge$ Equivalence class $a$ containing $a \in \mathrm{X}$ for an equivalence relation R in X is
the subset of X containing all elements $b$ related to $a$.
$\blacklozenge$ A function $f : \mathrm{X} \rightarrow$ is one-one (or injective) if

$f(x) \quad f(x) \Rightarrow x \quad x \quad \forall x, x \in \mathrm{X}$.
$\blacklozenge$ A function $f : \mathrm{X} \rightarrow$ is onto (or surjective) if given any $y \in$, $\exists x \in \mathrm{X}$ such
that $f(x) \quad y$.
$\blacklozenge$ A function $f : \mathrm{X} \rightarrow$ is one-one and onto (or bijective), if $f$ is both one-one
and onto.
$\blacklozenge$ The composition of functions $f : \mathrm{A} \rightarrow \mathrm{B}$ and $g : \mathrm{B} \rightarrow \mathrm{C}$ is the function
$gof : \mathrm{A} \rightarrow \mathrm{C}$ given by $gof(x) \quad g(f(x)) \forall x \in \mathrm{A}$.
$\blacklozenge$ A function $f : \mathrm{X} \rightarrow$ is invertible if $\exists g : \quad \rightarrow \mathrm{X}$ such that $gof \quad \mathrm{I}_x$ and
$fog \quad \mathrm{I}$.
$\blacklozenge$ A function $f : \mathrm{X} \rightarrow$ is invertible if and only if $f$ is one-one and onto.

<!-- page 45 -->
$\diamond$ iven a finite set X, a function $f: \mathrm{X} \rightarrow \mathrm{X}$ is one-one (respectively onto) if and
only if $f$ is onto (respectively one-one). This is the characteristic property of a
finite set. This is not true for infinite set
$\diamond$ A \textbf{\textit{binary operation}} $*$ on a set A is a function $*$ from $\mathrm{A} \times \mathrm{A}$ to $\mathrm{A}$.
$\diamond$ An element $e \in \mathrm{X}$ is the \textbf{\textit{identity}} element for binary operation $* : \mathrm{X} \times \mathrm{X} \rightarrow \mathrm{X}$,
if $a * e \quad a \quad e * a \quad \forall a \in \mathrm{X}$.
$\diamond$ An element $a \in \mathrm{X}$ is \textbf{\textit{invertible}} for binary operation $* : \mathrm{X} \times \mathrm{X} \rightarrow \mathrm{X}$, if
there exists $b \in \mathrm{X}$ such that $a * b \quad e \quad b * a$ where, $e$ is the identity for the
binary operation $*$. The element $b$ is called \textbf{\textit{inverse}} of $a$ and is denoted by $a$ .
$\diamond$ An operation $*$ on $\mathrm{X}$ is \textbf{\textit{commutative}} if $a * b \quad b * a \quad \forall a, b$ in $\mathrm{X}$.
$\diamond$ An operation $*$ on $\mathrm{X}$ is \textbf{\textit{associative}} if $(a * b) * c \quad a * (b * c) \forall a, b, c$ in $\mathrm{X}$.

Historical Note

The concept of function has evolved over a long period of time starting from
R. escartes (-), who used the word ‘function’ in his manuscript
$Geometrie$ in to mean some positive integral power $x^n$ of a variable $x$
while studying geometrical curves like hyperbola, parabola and ellipse. ames
regory (-) in his work $Vera$ $Circuli$ $et$ $Hyperbolae$ $Quadratura$
() considered function as a quantity obtained from other quantities by
successive use of algebraic operations or by any other operations. Later . .
Leibnit (-) in his manuscript $Methodus$ $tangentium$ $inversa$, $seu$ $de$
$functionibus$ written in used the word ‘function’ to mean a quantity varying
from point to point on a curve such as the coordinates of a point on the curve, the
slope of the curve, the tangent and the normal to the curve at a point. However,
in his manuscript $Historia$ (), Leibnit used the word ‘function’ to mean
quantities that depend on a variable. He was the first to use the phrase ‘function
of $x$’. ohn Bernoulli (-) used the notation $\phi x$ for the first time in to
indicate a function of $x$. But the general adoption of symbols like $f$, , $\phi$, $\psi$ ... to
represent functions was made by Leonhard Euler (-) in in the first
part of his manuscript $Analysis$ $Infinitorium$ . Later on, oeeph Louis Lagrange
(-) published his manuscripts $Theorie$ $des$ $functions$ $analytiques$ in
, where he discussed about analytic function and used the notion $f(x)$, ( $x$),
$\phi(x)$ etc. for different function of $x$. ubsequently, Lejeunne irichlet
(-) gave the definition of function which was being used till the set
theoretic definition of function presently used, was given after set theory was
developed by eorg Cantor (-). The set theoretic definition of function
known to us presently is simply an abstraction of the definition given by irichlet
in a rigorous manner.

INVERSE TRIGONOMETRIC
FUNCTIONS

❖ Mathematics, in general, is fundamentally the science of
self-evident things. — FELIX KLEIN ❖

2.1 Introduction

In Chapter 1, we have studied that the inverse of a function
$f$, denoted by $f^{-1}$, exists if $f$ is one-one and onto. There are
many functions which are not one-one, onto or both and
hence we can not talk of their inverses. In Class XI, we
studied that trigonometric functions are not one-one and
onto over their natural domains and ranges and hence their
inverses do not exist. In this chapter, we shall study about
the restrictions on domains and ranges of trigonometric
functions which ensure the existence of their inverses and
observe their behaviour through graphical representations.
Besides, some elementary properties will also be discussed.

The inverse trigonometric functions play an important
role in calculus for they serve to define many integrals.
The concepts of inverse trigonometric functions is also used in science and engineering.
                                                                 **Arya Bhatta**
                                                                 **(476-550 A. D.)**

2.2 Basic Concepts

In Class XI, we have studied trigonometric functions, which are defined as follows:
sine function, i.e., $\text{sine} : \mathbf{R} \rightarrow [-1, 1]$
cosine function, i.e., $\cos : \mathbf{R} \rightarrow [-1, 1]$

tangent function, i.e., $\tan : \mathbf{R} - \{ x : x = (2n + 1) \frac{\pi}{2}, n \in \mathbf{Z} \} \rightarrow \mathbf{R}$
cotangent function, i.e., $\cot : \mathbf{R} - \{ x : x = n\pi, n \in \mathbf{Z} \} \rightarrow \mathbf{R}$

secant function, i.e., $\sec : \mathbf{R} - \{ x : x = (2n + 1) \frac{\pi}{2}, n \in \mathbf{Z} \} \rightarrow \mathbf{R} - (-1, 1)$
cosecant function, i.e., $\text{cosec} : \mathbf{R} - \{ x : x = n\pi, n \in \mathbf{Z} \} \rightarrow \mathbf{R} - (-1, 1)$

and $y=f(x), y \in$. ere, the domain of $g=$ range of $f$ and the range of $g=$ domain
of $f$. The function $g$ is called the inverse of $f$ and is denoted by $f^{-1}$. urther, $g$ is also
one-one and onto and inverse of $g$ is $f$. Thus, $g^{-1}=\left(f^{-1}\right)^{-1}=f$. e also have
$$\left(f^{-1} \circ f\right)(x)=f^{-1}(f(x))=f^{-1}(y)=x$$
and $$(f \circ f^{-1})(y)=f\left(f^{-1}(y)\right)=f(x)=y$$
ince the domain of sine function is the set of all real numbers and range is the

rom the definition of the inverse functions, it follows that $\sin \left(\sin ^{-1} x\right)=x$

Note

1. $\sin^{-1}x$ should not be confused with $(\sin x)^{-1}$. In fact $(\sin x)^{-1} = \frac{1}{\sin x}$ and
similarly for other trigonometric functions.
2. henever no branch of an inverse trigonometric functions is mentioned, we
mean the principal value branch of that function.
. The value of an inverse trigonometric functions which lies in the range of
principal branch is called the principal value of that inverse trigonometric
functions.

e now consider some examples:

Example 1 ind the principal value of $\sin^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)$.

Solution et $\sin^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)=y$. Then, $\sin y=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$.

e know that the range of the principal value branch of $\sin^{-1}$ is $\left(\frac{-\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$ and

$\sin \left(\frac{\pi}{\underline{\pi}}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$. Therefore, principal value of $\sin^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)$ is $\frac{\pi}{\underline{\pi}}$

Example 2 ind the principal value of $\cot^{-1}\left(\frac{-1}{\sqrt{\underline{\pi}}}\right)$

Solution et $\cot^{-1}\left(\frac{-1}{\sqrt{\underline{\pi}}}\right)=y$. Then,

$$\cot y=\frac{-1}{\sqrt{\underline{\pi}}}=-\cot \left(\frac{\pi}{\underline{\pi}}\right)=\cot \left(\pi-\frac{\pi}{\underline{\pi}}\right)=\cot \left(\frac{2 \pi}{\underline{\pi}}\right)$$

e know that the range of principal value branch of $\cot^{-1}$ is $(,\pi)$ and

$$\cot \left(\frac{2 \pi}{\underline{\pi}}\right)=\frac{-1}{\sqrt{\underline{\pi}}}$. ence, principal value of $\cot^{-1}\left(\frac{-1}{\sqrt{\underline{\pi}}}\right)$ is $\frac{2 \pi}{\underline{\pi}}$$

2.3 Properties of Inverse Trigonometric Functions

In this section, we shall prove some important properties of inverse trigonometric
functions. It may be mentioned here that these results are valid within the principal
value branches of the corresponding inverse trigonometric functions and wherever
they are defined. ome results may not be valid for all values of the domains of inverse
trigonometric functions. In fact, they will be valid only for some values of $x$ for which
inverse trigonometric functions are defined. e will not go into the details of these
values of $x$ in the domain as this discussion goes beyond the scope of this text book.

et us recall that if $y = \sin^{-1}x$, then $x = \sin y$ and if $x = \sin y$, then $y = \sin^{-1}x$. This is
euivalent to

$\sin \left(\sin ^{-1} x\right)=x, x \in[-1,1]$ and $\sin ^{-1}(\sin x)=x, x \in \square\left[-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$

ame is true for other five inverse trigonometric functions as well. e now prove
some properties of inverse trigonometric functions.

1. (i) $\sin^{-1} \frac{1}{x} = \text{cosec}^{-1} x, x \geq 1$ or $x \leq -1$


(ii) $\cos^{-1} \frac{1}{x} = \text{sec}^{-1} x, x \geq 1$ or $x \leq -1$

(iii) $\tan^{-1} \frac{1}{x} = \cot^{-1} x, x > 0$
To prove the first result, we put $\text{cosec}^{-1} x = y$, i.e., $x = \text{cosec } y$

Therefore $\frac{1}{x} = \sin y$

ence $\sin^{-1} \frac{1}{x} = y$

or $\sin^{-1} \frac{1}{x} = \text{cosec}^{-1} x$

imilarly, we can prove the other parts.
2. (i) $\sin^{-1} (-x) = -\sin^{-1} x, x \in [-1, 1]$
(ii) $\tan^{-1} (-x) = -\tan^{-1} x, x \in \mathbf{R}$
(iii) $\text{cosec}^{-1} (-x) = -\text{cosec}^{-1} x, |x| \geq 1$
et $\sin^{-1} (-x) = y$, i.e., $-x = \sin y$ so that $x = -\sin y$, i.e., $x = \sin (-y)$.
ence $\sin^{-1} x = -y = -\sin^{-1} (-x)$
Therefore $\sin^{-1} (-x) = -\sin^{-1} x$

imilarly, we can prove the other parts.
3. (i) $\cos^{-1} (-x) = \pi - \cos^{-1} x, x \in [-1, 1]$
(ii) $\sec^{-1} (-x) = \pi - \sec^{-1} x, |x| \geq 1$
(iii) $\cot^{-1} (-x) = \pi - \cot^{-1} x, x \in \mathbf{R}$
et $\cos^{-1} (-x) = y$ i.e., $-x = \cos y$ so that $x = -\cos y = \cos (\pi - y)$
Therefore $\cos^{-1} x = \pi - y = \pi - \cos^{-1} (-x)$
ence $\cos^{-1} (-x) = \pi - \cos^{-1} x$
imilarly, we can prove the other parts.

4. (i) $\sin^{-1} x + \cos^{-1} x = \frac{\pi}{2}, x \in [-1, 1]$

(ii) $\tan^{-1} x + \cot^{-1} x = \frac{\pi}{2}, x \in \mathbf{R}$

(iii) $\text{cosec}^{-1} x + \sec^{-1} x = \frac{\pi}{2}, |x| \geq 1$

et $\sin^{-1} x = y$. Then $x = \sin y = \cos \left( \frac{\pi}{2} - y \right)$

Therefore $\cos^{-1} x = \frac{\pi}{2} - y = \frac{\pi}{2} - \sin^{-1} x$

<!-- page 58 -->
MTMTIC

ence $\sin^{-1} x + \cos^{-1} x = \frac{\pi}{2}$

imilarly, we can prove the other parts.

5. (i) $\tan^{-1}x + \tan^{-1}y = \tan^{-1} \frac{x+y}{1-xy}$ , $xy < 1$

(ii) $\tan^{-1}x - \tan^{-1}y = \tan^{-1} \frac{x-y}{1+xy}, xy > -1$

et $\tan^{-1} x = \theta$ and $\tan^{-1} y = \phi$. Then $x = \tan \theta$, $y = \tan \phi$

$$\text{ow} \qquad \tan(\theta + \phi) = \frac{\tan \theta + \tan \phi}{1 - \tan \theta \tan \phi} = \frac{x + y}{1 - xy}$$

This gives $\theta + \phi = \tan^{-1} \frac{x+y}{1-xy}$

ence $\tan^{-1} x + \tan^{-1} y = \tan^{-1} \frac{x+y}{1-xy}$

In the above result, if we replace $y$ by $-y$, we get the second result and by replacing
$y$ by $x$, we get the third result as given below.

6. (i) $2\tan^{-1} x = \sin^{-1} \frac{2x}{1+x^2}, |x| \le 1$


(ii) $2\tan^{-1} x = \cos^{-1} \frac{1-x^2}{1+x^2}, x \ge 0$


(iii) $2 \tan^{-1} x = \tan^{-1} \frac{2x}{1-x^2}, -1 < x < 1$


et $\tan^{-1} x = y$, then $x = \tan y$. ow


$$\sin^{-1} \frac{2x}{1+x^2} = \sin^{-1} \frac{2\tan y}{1+\tan^2 y}$$
$$= \sin^{-1} (\sin 2y) = 2y = 2\tan^{-1} x$$

$$\text{Iso cos}^{-1} \frac{1-x^2}{1+x^2} = \cos^{-1} \frac{1-\tan^2 y}{1+\tan^2 y} = \cos^{-1} (\cos 2y) = 2y = 2\tan^{-1} x$$

(iii) Can be worked out similarly.

Example 3 how that

Example 3 now that

(i) $\sin^{-1} \left(2x\sqrt{1-x^2}\right) = 2 \sin^{-1} x, -\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \le x \le \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$

(ii) $\sin^{-1} \left(2x\sqrt{1-x^2}\right) = 2 \cos^{-1} x, \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \le x \le 1$

Solution

(i) et $x = \sin \theta$. Then $\sin^{-1} x = \theta$. e have

$$\sin^{-1} \left(2x\sqrt{1-x^2}\right) = \sin^{-1} \left(2 \sin \theta \sqrt{1-\sin^2 \theta}\right)$$

$$= \sin^{-1} (2\sin\theta \cos\theta) = \sin^{-1} (\sin2\theta) = 2\theta$$

$$= 2 \sin^{-1} x$$

(ii) Take $x = \cos \theta$, then proceeding as above, we get, $\sin^{-1} \left(2x\sqrt{1-x^2}\right) = 2 \cos^{-1} x$

Example 4 how that $\tan^{-1} \frac{1}{2} + \tan^{-1} \frac{2}{11} = \tan^{-1} -$

Solution By property (i), we have

$$... = \tan^{-1} \frac{1}{2} + \tan^{-1} \frac{2}{11} = \tan^{-1} \frac{\frac{1}{2} + \frac{2}{11}}{1 - \frac{1}{2} \times \frac{2}{11}} = \tan^{-1} \frac{1}{2} = \tan^{-1} - = ...$$

Example 5 xpress $\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\cos x}{1-\sin x}\right)$, $-\frac{\pi}{2}<x<\frac{\pi}{2}$ in the simplest form.

Solution e write

$$\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\cos x}{1-\sin x}\right)=\tan^{-1}\left[\frac{\cos ^{2} \frac{x}{2}-\sin ^{2} \frac{x}{2}}{\cos ^{2} \frac{x}{2}+\sin ^{2} \frac{x}{2}-2 \sin \frac{x}{2} \cos \frac{x}{2}}\right]$$

<!-- page 60 -->
MTMTIC

$$= \tan^{-1} \left[ \frac{\left( \cos \frac{x}{2} + \sin \frac{x}{2} \right) \left( \cos \frac{x}{2} - \sin \frac{x}{2} \right)}{\left( \cos \frac{x}{2} - \sin \frac{x}{2} \right)^2} \right]$$

$$= \tan^{-1} \left[ \frac{\cos \frac{x}{2} + \sin \frac{x}{2}}{\cos \frac{x}{2} - \sin \frac{x}{2}} \right] = \tan^{-1} \left[ \frac{1 + \tan \frac{x}{2}}{1 - \tan \frac{x}{2}} \right]$$

$$= \tan^{-1} \left[ \tan \left( \frac{\pi}{2} + \frac{x}{2} \right) \right] = \frac{\pi}{2} + \frac{x}{2}$$

Alternatively,

$$\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\cos x}{1-\sin x}\right)=\tan^{-1}\left[\frac{\sin \left(\frac{\pi}{2}-x\right)}{1-\cos \left(\frac{\pi}{2}-x\right)}\right]=\tan^{-1}\left[\frac{\sin \left(\frac{\pi-2 x}{2}\right)}{1-\cos \left(\frac{\pi-2 x}{2}\right)}\right]$$

Example 6 rite $\cot^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{x^2-1}}\right)$, $x$ 1 in the simplest form.

$$= \tan^{-1} (\tan \theta) = \theta = \tan^{-1} x = \tan^{-1} x + 2 \tan^{-1} x$$

$$= \tan^{-1} x + \tan^{-1} \frac{2x}{1-x^2} = ... \text{ (hy)}$$

Summary

The domains and ranges (principal value branches) of inverse trigonometric
functions are given in the following table:

<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Functions</th>
<th>Domain</th>
<th>Range<br/>(Principal Value Branches)</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>y = \sin^{-1} x</td>
<td>[-1, 1]</td>
<td>\left[\frac{-\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>y = \cos^{-1} x</td>
<td>[-1, 1]</td>
<td>[, \pi]</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>y = \text{cosec}^{-1} x</td>
<td>\mathbf{R} - (-1, 1)</td>
<td>\left[\frac{-\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right] - \{\}</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>y = \sec^{-1} x</td>
<td>\mathbf{R} - (-1, 1)</td>
<td>[, \pi] - \{\frac{\pi}{2}\}</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>y = \tan^{-1} x</td>
<td>\mathbf{R}</td>
<td>\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>y = \cot^{-1} x</td>
<td>\mathbf{R}</td>
<td>(, \pi)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

$\diamond$ $\sin^{-1}x$ should not be confused with $(\sin x)^{-1}$. In fact $(\sin x)^{-1} = \frac{1}{\sin x}$ and
  similarly for other trigonometric functions.
$\diamond$ The value of an inverse trigonometric functions which lies in its principal
  value branch is called the *principal value* of that inverse trigonometric
  functions.

Historical Note

The study of trigonometry was first started in India. The ancient Indian
Mathematicians, ryabhatta (.), Brahmagupta ( .), Bhaskara I
(..) and Bhaskara II (111 ..) got important results of trigonometry. ll
this knowledge went from India to rabia and then from there to urope. The
reeks had also started the study of trigonometry but their approach was so
clumsy that when the Indian approach became known, it was immediately adopted
throughout the world.

In India, the predecessor of the modern trigonometric functions, known as
the sine of an angle, and the introduction of the sine function represents one of
the main contribution of the $siddhantas$ (anskrit astronomical works) to
mathematics.

Bhaskara I (about ..) gave formulae to find the values of sine functions
for angles more than . sixteenth century Malayalam work      $Yuktibhasa$
contains a proof for the expansion of $\sin ( + B)$. xact expression for sines or
cosines of 1, , , 2, etc., were given by Bhaskara II.

The symbols $\sin^{-1} x$, $\cos^{-1} x$, etc., for $\text{arc sin } x$, $\text{arc cos } x$, etc., were suggested
by the astronomer ir ohn .. ersehel (11) The name of Thales
(about B.C.) is invariably associated with height and distance problems. e
is credited with the determination of the height of a great pyramid in gypt by
measuring shadows of the pyramid and an auxiliary staff (or gnomon) of known

Chapter 3

MATRICES

❖ The essence of Mathematics lies in its freedom. — CANTOR ❖

3.1 Introduction

The knowledge of matrices is necessary in various branches of mathematics. Matrices
are one of the most powerful tools in mathematics. This mathematical tool simplifies
our work to a great extent when compared with other straight forward methods. The
evolution of concept of matrices is the result of an attempt to obtain compact and
simple methods of solving system of linear equations. Matrices are not only used as a
representation of the coefficients in system of linear equations, but utility of matrices
far exceeds that use. Matrix notation and operations are used in electronic spreadsheet
programs for personal computer, which in turn is used in different areas of business
and science like budgeting, sales projection, cost estimation, analysing the results of an
experiment etc. Also, many physical operations such as magnification, rotation and
reflection through a plane can be represented mathematically by matrices. Matrices
are also used in cryptography. This mathematical tool is not only used in certain branches
of sciences, but also in genetics, economics, sociology, modern psychology and industrial
management.

In this chapter, we shall find it interesting to become acquainted with the
fundamentals of matrix and matrix algebra.

3.2 Matrix

Suppose we wish to express the information that Radha has 15 notebooks. We may
express it as $[15]$ with the understanding that the number inside $[ ]$ is the number of
notebooks that Radha has. Now, if we have to express that Radha has 15 notebooks
and 6 pens. We may express it as $[15 \quad 6]$ with the understanding that first number
inside $[ ]$ is the number of notebooks while the other one is the number of pens possessed
by Radha. Let us now suppose that we wish to express the information of possession

respectively. Similarly, in the second arrangement, the entries in the first row represent
the number of notebooks possessed by Radha, $\square$au$\bar{\text{i}}$a and Simran, respectively. The
entries in the second row represent the number of pens possessed by Radha, $\square$au$\bar{\text{i}}$a
and Simran, respectively. An arrangement or display of the above kind is called a
$matrix$. $\square$ormally, we define matrix as $\square$

Definition 1 A matrix is an ordered rectangular array of numbers or functions. The
numbers or functions are called the elements or the entries of the matrix.

We denote matrices by capital letters. The following are some examples of matrices $\square$

$$\mathrm{A} = \begin{bmatrix} \square & \square & 5 \\ \square & \sqrt{5} \\ \square & 6 \end{bmatrix} , \square = \begin{bmatrix} \square + i & \square & -\frac{1}{\square} \\ \square 5 & \square 1 & \square \\ \sqrt{\square} & 5 & \frac{5}{\square} \end{bmatrix} , \mathrm{C} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 + x & x \square & \square \\ \cos x & \sin x + \square & \tan x \end{bmatrix}$$

In the above examples, the horizontal lines of elements are said to constitute, \textbf{\textit{rows}}
of the matrix and the vertical lines of elements are said to constitute, \textbf{\textit{columns}} of the
matrix. Thus A has $\square$rows and $\square$columns, $\square$ has $\square$rows and $\square$columns while C has $\square$
rows and $\square$columns.

3.2.1 Order of a matrix

A matrix having $m$ rows and $n$ columns is called a matrix of $order$ $m$ $\square n$ or simply $m$ $\square n$
matrix $\square$ read as an $m$ by $n$ matrix $\square$ So referring to the above examples of matrices, we
have A as $\square\square\square$ matrix, $\square$ as $\square\square\square$ matrix and C as $\square\square\square$ matrix. We observe that A has
$\square\square\square\square\square$ 6 elements, $\square$ and C have $\square$ and 6 elements, respectively.

In general, an $m$ $\square n$ matrix has the following rectangular array $\square$

$$\begin{bmatrix} \\ a_{11} & a_{12} & a_{13} \cdots a_{1j} \cdots a_{1n} \\ \\ a_{21} & a_{22} & a_{23} \cdots a_{2j} \cdots a_{2n} \\ \\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\ \\ a_{i1} & a_{i2} & a_{i3} \cdots a_{ij} \cdots a_{im} \\ \\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\ \\ a_{m1} & a_{m2} & a_{m3} \cdots a_{mj} \cdots a_{mn} \\ \end{bmatrix}_{m \times n}$$

or A $\square [a_{ij}]_{m \square n}$, $1 \le i \le m$, $1 \le j \le n$ $i, j \in \mathrm{N}$

Thus the $i^{\text{th}}$ row consists of the elements $a_{i1}, a_{i2}, a_{i3},..., a_{in}$, while the $j^{\text{th}}$ column
consists of the elements $a_{1j}, a_{j2}, a_{j3},..., a_{mj}$,

In general $a_{ij}$, is an element lying in the $i^{\text{th}}$ row and $j^{\text{th}}$ column. We can also call
it as the $\boxminus, j^{\boxplus}$ element of A. The number of elements in an $m$ $\boxminus n$ matrix will be
equal to $mn$.

<!-- page 73 -->
Note In this chapter

1. We shall follow the notation, namely A $\Box [a_{ij}]_{m \cap n}$ to indicate that A is a matrix
of order $m \Box n$.

$\Box$ We shall consider only those matrices whose elements are real numbers or
functions taking real values.

We can also represent any point $\boxminus x, y \boxminus$ in a plane by a matrix $\boxminus$ column or row $\boxminus$ as

$\begin{bmatrix} x \\ y \end{bmatrix}$ or $[x, y] \boxminus \boxminus$ or example point $\boxminus \boxminus \boxminus$, $1$ as a matrix representation may be given as

$$\square = \begin{bmatrix} \square \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} \text{ or } [\square 1].$$

□bserve that in this way we can also express the vertices of a closed rectilinear
figure in the form of a matrix. □or example, consider a quadrilateral $A$ □C □ with vertices
$A$ □, □□, □□□, □□, C □1, □□, □ □□1, □□

Now, quadrilateral $A \square C \square$ in the matrix form, can be represented as

$$\begin{matrix} \\ & \text{A} & \square & \text{C} & \square \\ \\ \square = & \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square & 1 & -1 \\ \square & \square & \square & \square \end{bmatrix}_{\square \times \square} \\ \\ & & & & \\ \end{matrix} \quad \text{or} \quad \begin{matrix} \\ & \text{A} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square \\ \square & \square & \square \\ \text{C} & 1 & \square \\ \square & -1 & \square \end{bmatrix}_{\square \times \square} \\ \\ & \\ \\ \square = \\ \end{matrix}$$

Thus, matrices can be used as representation of vertices of geometrical figures in
a plane.

Example 2 If a matrix has $\square$ elements, what are the possible orders it can have $\square$

Example 2 If a matrix has $\square$ elements, what are the possible orders it can have $\square$

Solution We know that if a matrix is of order $m$ $\square n$, it has $mn$ elements. Thus, to find
all possible orders of a matrix with $\square$ elements, we will find all ordered pairs of natural
numbers, whose product is $\square$
Thus, all possible ordered pairs are $\square 1, \square \square, \square \square, 1 \square \square \square, \square \square \square \square \square$

Hence, possible orders are $1$ $\square \square \square \square \square 1, \square \square \square \square \square \square \square$

Example 3 Construct a $\square \square \square \square$ matrix whose elements are given by $a_{ij} = \frac{1}{\square} \square i - \square j \square$

Solution In general a $\square \square \square \square$ matrix is given by $\text{A} = \begin{bmatrix} a_{11} & a_{1 \square} \\ a_{\square} & a_{\square \square} \\ a_{\square} & a_{\square \square} \end{bmatrix}$.

Now $a_{ij} = \frac{1}{\square} \square i - \square j, i \square 1, \square \square \square$ and $j \square 1, \square$

Therefore $a_{11} = \frac{1}{\square} \square 1 - \square \times 1 \square \mp 1 \quad a_{1 \square} = \frac{1}{\square} \square 1 - \square \times \square = \frac{5}{\square}$

$a_{\square} = \frac{1}{\square} \square \square - \square \times 1 = \frac{1}{\square} \quad a_{\square} = \frac{1}{\square} \square - \square \times \square \square \mp \square$

$a_{\square} = \frac{1}{\square} \square \square - \square \times 1 \square \mp \square \quad a_{\square} = \frac{1}{\square} \square - \square \times \square = \frac{\square}{\square}$

Hence the required matrix is given by $\text{A} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \frac{5}{\square} \\ \frac{1}{\square} & \square \\ \square & \frac{\square}{\square} \end{bmatrix}$.

<!-- page 75 -->
3.3 Types of Matrices

In this section, we shall discuss different types of matrices.

ii Column matrix
A matrix is said to be a $column$ $matrix$ if it has only one column.

$\square$or example, $\text{A} = \begin{bmatrix} \square \\ \sqrt{\square} \\ -1 \\ 1 \square \end{bmatrix}$ is a column matrix of order $\square \square 1$.

In general, $\text{A} \square [a_{ij}]_{m \square 1}$ is a column matrix of order $m \square 1$.
iii Column matrix
A matrix is said to be a $row$ $matrix$ if it has only one row.

$\square$or example, $\square = \begin{bmatrix} -\frac{1}{\square} & \sqrt{5} & \square & \square \\ \square & \square & \square & \square \end{bmatrix}_{1 \square n}$ is a row matrix.

In general, $\square \square [b_{ij}]_{1 \square n}$ is a row matrix of order $1 \square n$.
iii Square matrix
A matrix in which the number of rows are equal to the number of columns, is
said to be a $square$ $matrix$. Thus an $m \square n$ matrix is said to be a square matrix if
$m \square n$ and is known as a square matrix of order $\square n$

$\square$or example $\text{A} = \begin{bmatrix} \square & -1 & \square \\ -\square & \square \sqrt{\square} & 1 \\ \square & \square & -1 \end{bmatrix}$ is a square matrix of order $\square$

In general, $\text{A} \square [a_{ij}]_{m \square m}$ is a square matrix of order $m$.

$\text{Note}$ If $\text{A} \square [a_{ij}]$ is a square matrix of order $n$, then elements entries $\square a_{11}, a_{12}, ..., a_{m}$

are said to constitute the $diagonal$, of the matrix $\text{A}$. Thus, if $\text{A} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -\square & 1 \\ \square & \square & -1 \\ \square & 5 & 6 \end{bmatrix}$.

Then the elements of the diagonal of $\text{A}$ are $1, \square, 6$.

6 ☐ MATHEMATICS

iv$\square$ **Diagonal matrix**

A square matrix $\square \square [b_{ij}]_{m \square m}$ is said to be a *diagonal matrix* if all its non
diagonal elements are $\square$ero, that is a matrix $\square \square [b_{ij}]_{m \square m}$ is said to be a diagonal
matrix if $b_{ij} \square \square$, when $i \neq j$.

$\square$or example, A $\square [\square]$, $\square = \begin{bmatrix} -1 & \square \\ \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$, C = $\begin{bmatrix} -1.1 & \square & \square \\ \square & \square & \square \\ \square & \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$, are diagonal matrices

of order 1, $\square \square$ respectively.
$\square$v$\square$ **Scalar matrix**

A diagonal matrix is said to be a *scalar matrix* if its diagonal elements are equal,
that is, a square matrix $\square \square [b_{ij}]_{n \square n}$ is said to be a scalar matrix if
$$b_{ij} \square \square \text{ when } i \neq j$$
$$b_{ij} \square k, \text{ when } i \square j, \text{ for some constant } k.$$
$\square$or example

A $\square [\square]$, $\square = \begin{bmatrix} -1 & \square \\ \square & -1 \end{bmatrix}$, C = $\begin{bmatrix} \sqrt{\square} & \square & \square \\ \square & \sqrt{\square} & \square \\ \square & \square & \sqrt{\square} \end{bmatrix}$

are scalar matrices of order 1, $\square$and $\square$ respectively.
$\square$vi$\square$ **Identity matrix**

A square matrix in which elements in the diagonal are all 1 and rest are all $\square$ero
is called an *identity matrix*. In other words, the square matrix A $\square [a_{ij}]_{n \square n}$ is an

identity matrix, if $a_{ij} = \begin{cases} 1 & \text{if } i = j \\ \square & \text{if } i \neq j \end{cases}$.

We denote the identity matrix of order $n$ by I$_n$. When order is clear from the
context, we simply write it as I.

$\square$or example [1], $\begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square \\ \square & 1 \end{bmatrix}$, $\begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square & \square \\ \square & 1 & \square \\ \square & \square & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ are identity matrices of order 1, $\square$and $\square$

respectively.
$\square$bserve that a scalar matrix is an identity matrix when $k \square 1$. $\square$ut every identity
matrix is clearly a scalar matrix.

<!-- page 77 -->
vii $\square$ **Zero matrix**
A matrix is said to be *zero matrix* or *null matrix* if all its elements are $\square$ero.

$\square$or example, $[\square]$, $\begin{bmatrix} \square & \square \\ \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$, $\begin{bmatrix} \square & \square & \square \\ \square & \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$, $[\square, \square]$ are all $\square$ero matrices. We denote
$\square$ero matrix by $\square$. Its order will be clear from the context.

3.3.1 Equality of matrices

**Definition 2** Two matrices A $\square [a_{ij}]$ and $\square \square [b_{ij}]$ are said to be equal if
i $\square$ they are of the same order
ii $\square$ each element of A is equal to the corresponding element of $\square$, that is $a_{ij} \square b_{ij}$ for
all $i$ and $j$.

$\square$ or example, $\begin{bmatrix} \square & \square \\ \square & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ and $\begin{bmatrix} \square & \square \\ \square & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ are equal matrices but $\begin{bmatrix} \square & \square \\ \square & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ and $\begin{bmatrix} \square & \square \\ \square & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ are

not equal matrices. Symbolically, if two matrices $A$ and $\square$ are equal, we write $A \begin{array}{|c|} \hline \hline \end{array} \square$.

If $\begin{bmatrix} x & y \\ z & a \\ b & c \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -1.5 & \square \\ \square & \sqrt{6} \\ \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$, then $x \square \square \square 1.5, y \square \square \square z \square \square \square a \square \sqrt{6}, b \square \square \square c \square \square$

Example 4 If $\begin{bmatrix} x + \square & z + \square & \square y - \square \\ -6 & a - 1 & \square \\ b - \square & -\square 1 & \square \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square & 6 & \square y - \square \\ -6 & -\square & \square c + \square \\ \square b + \square & -\square 1 & \square \end{bmatrix}$

$\square$ind the values of $a, b, c, x, y$ and $z$.

Solution As the given matrices are equal, therefore, their corresponding elements
must be equal. Comparing the corresponding elements, we get

$x \square \square \square \square, \quad z \square \square \square 6, \quad \square y \square \square \square y \square \square$
$a \square 1 \square \square \square, \quad \square \square \square c \square \square \quad b \square \square \square c \square \square,$

Simplifying, we get

$$a \square \square \square, b \square \square \square, c \square \square 1, x \square \square \square, y \square \square 5, z \square \square$$

Example 5 $\square$ind the values of $a$, $b$, $c$, and $d$ from the following equation $\square$

$$\begin{bmatrix} \\ \square a + b & a - \square b \\ \\ 5c - d & \square c + \square d \\ \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \\ \square & -\square \\ \\ 11 & \square \\ \end{bmatrix}$$

<!-- page 78 -->
Solution $\square$y equality of two matrices, equating the corresponding elements, we get

Solving these equations, we get
$$a \square 1, b \square \square, c \square \square \text{and } d \square \square$$

3.4 Operations on Matrices

In this section, we shall introduce certain operations on matrices, namely, addition of
matrices, multiplication of a matrix by a scalar, difference and multiplication of matrices.

Suppose $\square$atima wants to know the total production of sport shoes in each price
category. Then the total production

In category 1 $\square$ for boys $\boxdot\boxdot\boxdot\boxdot\boxdot\boxdot$, for girls $\boxdot6\boxdot\square5\square$

In category $\square\square$ for boys $\boxdot5\square\boxdot\boxdot\boxdot$, for girls $\boxdot65\square55\square$

In category $\square\square$ for boys $\boxdot\boxdot\boxdot\square5\square$ for girls $\boxdot5\square\square5\square$

This can be represented in the matrix form as $\begin{bmatrix} \square\square + \square\square & 6\square + 5\square \\ \square 5 + \square\square & 65 + 55 \\ \square\square + \square 5 & \square 5 + \square 5 \end{bmatrix}$.

<!-- page 80 -->
This new matrix is the **sum** of the above two matrices. We observe that the sum of
two matrices is a matrix obtained by adding the corresponding elements of the given
matrices. $\square$Furthermore, the two matrices have to be of the same order.

Thus, if $A = \begin{bmatrix} a_{11} & a_{1\Box} & a_{1\Box} \\ a_{\Box} & a_{\Box} & a_{\Box} \end{bmatrix}$ is a $\Box\Box\Box$ matrix and $\Box = \begin{bmatrix} b_{11} & b_{1\Box} & b_{1\Box} \\ b_{\Box} & b_{\Box} & b_{\Box} \end{bmatrix}$ is another

Matrix. Then, we define A $\square\square=\begin{bmatrix} a_{11}+b_{11} & a_{1\square}+b_{1\square} & a_{1\square}+b_{1\square} \\ a_{\square}+b_{\square} & a_{\square}+b_{\square} & a_{\square}+b_{\square} \end{bmatrix}$.

In general, if A $\square [a_{ij}]$ and $\square \square [b_{ij}]$ are two matrices of the same order, say $m \square n$.
Then, the sum of the two matrices A and $\square$ is $defined$ as a matrix C $\square [c_{ij}]_{m \square n}$, where
$c_{ij} \square a_{ij} \square b_{ij}$, for all possible values of $i$ and $j$.

Example 6 $\square$iven $A = \begin{bmatrix} \sqrt{\square} & 1 & -1 \\ \square & \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$ and $\square = \begin{bmatrix} \square & \sqrt{5} & 1 \\ -\square & \square & \frac{1}{\square} \end{bmatrix}$, find $A$ $\square \square$

Since A, $\square$ are of the same order $\square\square\square$ Therefore, addition of A and $\square$ is defined
and is given by

$$A \begin{array}{|c|} \\ \hline \\ \end{array} = \begin{bmatrix} \\ \square+\sqrt{\square} & 1+\sqrt{5} & 1-1 \\ \\ \square-\square & \square+\square & \square+\frac{1}{\square} \\ \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \\ \square+\sqrt{\square} & 1+\sqrt{5} & \square \\ \\ \square & 6 & \frac{1}{\square} \\ \end{bmatrix}$$

Note

1. We emphasise that if A and $\square$ are not of the same order, then A $\square\square$ is not

defined. $\square$ or example if $A = \begin{bmatrix} \square & \square \\ 1 & \square \end{bmatrix}$, $\square = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square & \square \\ 1 & \square & 1 \end{bmatrix}$, then A $\square\square$ is not defined.

$\square$ We may observe that addition of matrices is an example of binary operation
on the set of matrices of the same order.

In general, we may define *multiplication of a matrix* by a scalar as follows $\boxminus$ if
$\mathrm{A} \boxminus [a_{ij}]_{m \ominus n}$ is a matrix and $k$ is a scalar, then $kA$ is another matrix which is obtained
by multiplying each element of $\mathrm{A}$ by the scalar $k$.

In other words, $kA$ $\square k[a_{ij}]_{m \square n}$ $\square [k \overline{a}_{ij}]_{m \square n}$, that is, $\overline{i}, j \rightleftharpoons$ element of $kA$ is $ka_{ij}$
for all possible values of $i$ and $j$.

$\square$ or example, if $\quad$ A $\square \begin{bmatrix} \square & 1 & 1.5 \\ \sqrt{5} & \square & -\square \\ \square & \square & 5 \end{bmatrix}$, then

$$\square \text{A } \square \square \begin{bmatrix} \square & 1 & 1.5 \\ \sqrt{5} & \square & -\square \\ \square & \square & 5 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square & \square & \square 5 \\ \square \sqrt{5} & \square & -\square \\ 6 & \square & 15 \end{bmatrix}$$

**Difference of matrices** If A $\square [a_{ij}]$, $\square \square [b_{ij}]$ are two matrices of the same order,
say $m \square n$, then difference A $\square \square$ is defined as a matrix $\square \square [d_{ij}]$, where $d_{ij} \square a_{ij} \square b_{ij}$,
for all value of $i$ and $j$. In other words, $\square \square \text{A} \square \square \square \text{A} \square \square \text{I} \square \square$, that is sum of the matrix
A and the matrix $\square \square$.

**Example 7** If $A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square & \square \\ \square & \square & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ and $\square = \begin{bmatrix} \square & -1 & \square \\ -1 & \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$, then find $\square A \square \square$.

**Solution We have**

$$\square A \square \square \square \square \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square & \square \\ \square & \square & 1 \end{bmatrix} - \begin{bmatrix} \square & -1 & \square \\ -1 & \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$$

$$\square \begin{bmatrix} \square & \square & 6 \\ \square & 6 & \square \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} -\square & 1 & -\square \\ 1 & \square & -\square \end{bmatrix}$$

$$\square \begin{bmatrix} \square - \square & \square + 1 & 6 - \square \\ \square + 1 & 6 + \square & \square - \square \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -1 & 5 & \square \\ 5 & 6 & \square \end{bmatrix}$$

3.4.3 Properties of matrix addition

The addition of matrices satisfy the following properties $\square$

ii $\square$ **Commutative Law** If A $\square [a_{ij}]$, $\square \square [b_{ij}]$ are matrices of the same order, say
$m \square n$, then A $\square \square \square \square \square \square$ A.
Now $\quad$ A $\square \square \square [a_{ij}] \square [b_{ij}] \square [a_{ij} \square b_{ij}]$
$\square [b_{ij} \square a_{ij}]$ addition of numbers is commutative $\square$
$\square [b_{ij}] \square [a_{ij}] \square \square \square \square \square$ A
iii $\square$ **Associative Law** $\square$ or any three matrices A $\square [a_{ij}]$, $\square \square [b_{ij}]$, C $\square [c_{ij}]$ of the
same order, say $m \square n$, A $\square \square \square \square$ C $\square$ A $\square \square \square \square$ C $\square$
Now $\quad$ A $\square \square \square \square$ C $\square [a_{ij}] \square [b_{ij}] \square \square [c_{ij}]$
$\square [a_{ij} \square b_{ij}] \square [c_{ij}] \square [a_{ij} \square b_{ij} \square \square c_{ij}]$
$\square [a_{ij} \square b_{ij} \square c_{ij}]$ $\quad$ Why $\square$
$\square [a_{ij}] \square [b_{ij} \square c_{ij}] \square [a_{ij}] \square [b_{ij}] \square [c_{ij}] \square \square$ A $\square \square \square \square$ C $\square$

<!-- page 83 -->
$\text{iii}\square$ **Existence of additive identity** Let $\text{A} \square [a_{ij}]$ be an $m \square n$ matrix and
$\square$ be an $m \square n$ zero matrix, then $\text{A} \square \square \square \square \square \square \text{A} \square \text{A}$. In other words, $\square$ is the
additive identity for matrix addition.

$\text{iv}\square$ **The existence of additive inverse** Let $\text{A} \square [a_{ij}]_{m \square n}$ be any matrix, then we
have another matrix as $\square \text{A} \square [\square a_{ij}]_{m \square n}$ such that $\text{A} \square \square \text{A} \square \square \square \text{A} \square \square \text{A} \square \square \square$. So
$\square \text{A}$ is the additive inverse of $\text{A}$ or negative of $\text{A}$.

3.4.4 Properties of scalar multiplication of a matrix

If A $\square [a_{ij}]$ and $\square \square [b_{ij}]$ be two matrices of the same order, say $m \square n$, and $k$ and $l$ are
scalars, then

- $\text{i} \square \quad k \sqcap \mathbf{A} \quad \square \square \square \square \quad k \ \mathbf{A} \quad \square \ k \square$, $\text{ii} \square \quad k \quad \square \ l \sqcap \mathbf{A} \quad \square \ k \ \mathbf{A} \quad \square \ l \ \mathbf{A}$
- $\text{iii} \square \quad k \ \sqcap \ \mathbf{A} \quad \square \square \square \square \quad k \ \sqcap [a_{ij}] \quad \square \ [b_{ij}] \square$
$\quad \quad \quad \quad \quad \quad \square \ k \ [a_{ij} \quad \square \ b_{ij}] \quad \square \ [k \ \cancel{a}_{ij} \quad \square \ b_{ij}] \quad \square \ [k \ a_{ij} \square \square \ k \ b_{ij}]$
$\quad \quad \quad \quad \quad \quad \square \ [k \ a_{ij}] \quad \square \ [k \ b_{ij}] \quad \square \ k \ [a_{ij}] \quad \square \ k \ [b_{ij}] \quad \square \ k \mathbf{A} \quad \square \ k \square$
- $\text{iii} \square \quad k \quad \square \ l \square \mathbf{A} \quad \square \ \cancel{k} \quad \square \ l \square [a_{ij}]$
$\quad \quad \quad \quad \quad \quad \square \ [k \quad \square \ l \square a_{ij}] \quad \square \ [k \ a_{ij}] \quad \square \ [l \ a_{ij}] \quad \square \ k \ [a_{ij}] \quad \square \ l \ [a_{ij}] \quad \square \ k \ \mathbf{A} \quad \square \ l \ \mathbf{A}$

Example 9 $\square$ind $\square$ and $\square$, if $\square + \square = \begin{bmatrix} 5 & \square \\ \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$ and $\square - \square = \begin{bmatrix} \square & 6 \\ \square & -1 \end{bmatrix}$.

Example 10 $\square$ind the values of $x$ and $y$ from the following equation$\square$

$$\square \begin{bmatrix} x & 5 \\ \square & y - \square \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} \square & -\square \\ 1 & \square \end{bmatrix} \square \begin{bmatrix} \square & 6 \\ 15 & 1 \square \end{bmatrix}$$

Example 11 Two farmers Ramkishan and $\square$urcharan Singh cultivates only three
varieties of rice namely $\square$asmati, $\square$ermal and Naura. The sale $\square$in Rupees$\square$of these
varieties of rice by both the farmers in the month of September and $\square$ctober are given
by the following matrices A and $\square$.

September Sales (in Rupees)
$$A = \begin{bmatrix} \text{Basmati} & \text{Permal} & \text{Naura} \\ 10,000 & 20,000 & 30,000 \\ 50,000 & 30,000 & 10,000 \end{bmatrix} \begin{matrix} \text{Ramkishan} \\ \text{Gurcharan Singh} \end{matrix}$$

October Sales (in Rupees)

3.4.5 Multiplication of matrices

Suppose Meera and Nadeem are two friends. Meera wants to buy $\square$pens and 5 story
books, while Nadeem needs $\square$pens and 1$\square$story books. They both go to a shop to
enquire about the rates which are quoted as follows$\square$

$\square$en $\square$Rs 5 each, story book $\square$Rs 5 $\square$each.

How much money does each need to spend $\square$Clearly, Meera needs Rs 5 $\square$ 5 $\square$ 5 $\square$ 5 $\square$
that is Rs 6 $\square$ while Nadeem needs 5 $\square$ 5 $\square$ 1 $\square$ Rs, that is Rs 5 $\square$ In terms of
matrix representation, we can write the above information as follows $\square$

Requirements Prices per piece (in Rupees) Money needed (in Rupees)

$$\begin{bmatrix} \square & 5 \\ \square & 1 \square \end{bmatrix} \qquad \qquad \qquad \begin{bmatrix} 5 \\ 5 \square \end{bmatrix} \qquad \qquad \qquad \begin{bmatrix} 5 \times \square + 5 \times 5 \square \\ \square \times 5 + 1 \square \times 5 \square \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square 6 \square \\ 5 \square \square \end{bmatrix}$$

Suppose that they enquire about the rates from another shop, quoted as follows $\square$
pen $\square$Rs $\square$each, story book $\square$Rs $\boxdot$each.

Now, the money required by Meera and Nadeem to make purchases will be
respectively Rs [Image of 5000 രൂപ] and Rs [Image of 1000 രൂപ]

<!-- page 87 -->
MATRICES

Again, the above information can be represented as follows $\square$

Requirements Prices per piece (in Rupees) Money needed (in Rupees)

$$\begin{bmatrix} \square & 5 \\ \square & 1 \square \end{bmatrix} \qquad \qquad \qquad \begin{bmatrix} \square \\ \square \end{bmatrix} \qquad \qquad \qquad \begin{bmatrix} \square \times \square + \square \times 5 \\ \square \times \square + 1 \times \square \times \square \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square \square \\ \square \square \end{bmatrix}$$

Now, the information in both the cases can be combined and expressed in terms of
matrices as follows $\square$

Requirements Prices per piece (in Rupees) Money needed (in Rupees)

$$\begin{bmatrix} \square & 5 \\ \square & 1 \square \end{bmatrix} \qquad \qquad \qquad \begin{bmatrix} 5 & \square \\ 5 \square & \square \end{bmatrix} \qquad \qquad \qquad \begin{bmatrix} 5 \times \square + 5 \times 5 \square & \square \times \square + \square \times 5 \\ \square \times 5 + 1 \square \times 5 \square & \square \times \square + 1 \square \times \square \square \end{bmatrix}$$

$$\qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \square \begin{bmatrix} \square 6 \square & \square \square \\ 5 \square & \square \square \end{bmatrix}$$

The above is an example of multiplication of matrices. We observe that, for
multiplication of two matrices A and $\square$, the number of columns in A should be equal to
the number of rows in $\square$. $\square$urthermore for getting the elements of the product matrix,
we take rows of A and columns of $\square$, multiply them element$\boxminus$wise and take the sum.
$\square$ormally, we define multiplication of matrices as follows$\square$

The $product$ of two matrices A and $\square$ is $defined$ if the number of columns of A is
equal to the number of rows of $\square$. Let A $\square[a_{ij}]$ be an $m$ $\square n$ matrix and $\square \square [b_{jk}]$ be an
$n$ $\square p$ matrix. Then the product of the matrices A and $\square$ is the matrix C of order $m$ $\square p$.
To get the $\bar{i}$, $k^{\text{th}}$ element $c_{ik}$ of the matrix C, we take the $i^{\text{th}}$ row of A and $k^{\text{th}}$ column
of $\square$, multiply them elementwise and take the sum of all these products. In other words,
if A $\square[a_{ij}]_{m \square p}$, $\square \square [b_{jk}]_{n \square p}$, then the $i^{\text{th}}$ row of A is $[a_{i1} \ a_{i2} \dots \ a_{in}]$ and the $k^{\text{th}}$ column of

$\square$ is $\begin{bmatrix} b_{1k} \\ b_{\cancel{1}k} \\ \vdots \\ b_{nk} \end{bmatrix}$, then $c_{ik} \square a_{i1} b_{1k} \square a_{i\square} b_{\cancel{1}k} \square a_{i\square} b_{\cancel{1}k} \square \dots \square a_{in} b_{nk} \square \sum_{j=1}^{n} a_{ij} b_{jk}$ .

The matrix C $\square [c_{ik}]_{m \square p}$ is the product of A and $\square$.

$\square$or example, if $C = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -1 & \square \\ \square & \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$ and $\square = \begin{bmatrix} \square & \square \\ -1 & 1 \\ 5 & -\square \end{bmatrix}$, then the product $C \square$ is defined

<!-- page 88 -->
and is given by $C\square=\begin{bmatrix}1 & -1 & \square \\ \square & \square & \square\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\square & \square \\ -1 & 1 \\ 5 & -\square\end{bmatrix}$. This is a $\square\square\square\square$ matrix in which each

entry is the sum of the products across some row of C with the corresponding entries
down some column of $\square$. These four computations are

Entry in
first row
first column
$$\begin{bmatrix} 1 & -1 & 2 \\ 0 & 3 & 4 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 2 & 7 \\ -1 & 1 \\ 5 & -4 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} (1) (2) + (-1) (-1) + (2) (5) & ? \\ ? & ? \end{bmatrix}$$

Entry in
first row
second column $\begin{bmatrix} 1 & -1 & 2 \\ 0 & 3 & 4 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 2 & 7 \\ -1 & 1 \\ 5 & -4 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 13 & (1)(7) + (-1)(1) + 2(-4) \\ ? & ? \end{bmatrix}$

second row

Entry in
second row

$\begin{bmatrix} 1 & -1 & 2 \\ 0 & 3 & 4 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 2 & 7 \\ -1 & 1 \\ 5 & -4 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 13 & -2 \\ 17 & 0(7) + 3(1) + 4(-4) \end{bmatrix}$

Thus $C \square = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \square & - \square \\ 1 \square & - 1 \square \end{bmatrix}$

Non-commutativity of multiplication of matrices

Now, we shall see by an example that even if $A \square$ and $\square A$ are both defined, it is not
necessary that $A \square \ \square \ \square A$.

Example 13 If $A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -\square & \square \\ -\square & \square & 5 \end{bmatrix}$ and $\square = \begin{bmatrix} \square & \square \\ \square & 5 \\ \square & 1 \end{bmatrix}$, then find $A\square$, $\square A$. Show that

$$\mathbf{A} \square \neq \square \mathbf{A}.$$

Solution Since A is a $\square\square\square$matrix and $\square$ is $\square\square\square$matrix. Hence A$\square$ and $\square$A are both
defined and are matrices of order $\square\square\square$and $\square\square\square$, respectively. Note that

$$A\square = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -\square & \square \\ -\square & \square & 5 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \square & \square \\ \square & 5 \\ \square & 1 \end{bmatrix} \square \begin{bmatrix} \square - \square + 6 & -\square - 1 \square + \square \\ -\square + \square + 1 \square & -1 \square + 1 \square + 5 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square & -\square \\ 1 \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$$

and $\square A = \begin{bmatrix} \square & \square \\ \square & 5 \\ \square & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -\square & \square \\ -\square & \square & 5 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square - 1 \square & -\square + 6 & 6 + 15 \\ \square - \square & -\square + 1 \square & 1 \square + \square 5 \\ \square - \square & -\square + \square & 6 + 5 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -1 \square & \square & \square \\ -16 & \square & \square \\ -\square & -\square & 11 \end{bmatrix}$$

Clearly $A\square \neq \square A$

In the above example both A$\square$ and $\square A$ are of different order and so A$\square \neq \square A$. $\square$ut
one may think that perhaps A$\square$ and $\square A$ could be the same if they were of the same
order. $\square$ut it is not so, here we give an example to show that even if A$\square$and $\square A$ are of
same order they may not be same.

Example 14 If $A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square \\ \square & -1 \end{bmatrix}$ and $\square = \begin{bmatrix} \square & 1 \\ 1 & \square \end{bmatrix}$, then $A \square = \begin{bmatrix} \square & 1 \\ -1 & \square \end{bmatrix}$.


and $\square A = \begin{bmatrix} \square & -1 \\ 1 & \square \end{bmatrix}$. Clearly $A \square \neq \square A$.


Thus matrix multiplication is not commutative.

Zero matrix as the product of two non zero matrices

We know that, for real numbers $a, b$ if $ab \square\square$, then either $a \square\square$ or $b \square\square$ This need

Example 15 $\square$ind A$\square$, if $A = \begin{bmatrix} \square & -1 \\ \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$ and $\square = \begin{bmatrix} \square & 5 \\ \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$.


Solution We have $A\square = \begin{bmatrix} \square & -1 \\ \square & \square \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \square & 5 \\ \square & \square \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square & \square \\ \square & \square \end{bmatrix}$.


Thus, if the product of two matrices is a $\square$ero matrix, it is not necessary that one of
the matrices is a $\square$ero matrix.

3.4.6 Properties of multiplication of matrices

The multiplication of matrices possesses the following properties, which we state without
proof.

1. **The associative law** $\square$ or any three matrices A, $\square$ and C. We have
$\text{A} \square \text{C} \square \text{A} \text{C}$, whenever both sides of the equality are defined.

$\square$ **The distributive law** $\square$ or three matrices A, $\square$ and C.

$\text{i} \square \text{A} \text{C} \square \text{A} \square \text{AC}$

$\text{ii} \square \text{A} \text{C} \square \text{AC} \square \text{C}$, whenever both sides of equality are defined.

$\square$ **The existence of multiplicative identity** $\square$ or every square matrix A, there
exist an identity matrix of same order such that IA $\square$ AI $\square$ A.

Now, we shall verify these properties by examples.

Example 16 If $A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & -1 \\ \square & \square & \square \\ \square & -1 & \square \end{bmatrix}$, $\square = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square \\ \square & \square \\ -1 & \square \end{bmatrix}$ and $C = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square & \square & -\square \\ \square & \square & -\square & 1 \end{bmatrix}$, find

$A \square C \square A \square C$ and show that $\square A \square C \square A \square C \square$

Solution We have $A \square = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & -1 \\ \square & \square & \square \\ \square & -1 & \square \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square \\ \square & \square \\ -1 & \square \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 + \square + 1 & \square + \square - \square \\ \square + \square - \square & 6 + \square + 1 \square \\ \square + \square - \square & \square - \square + \square \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square & 1 \\ -1 & 1 \square \\ 1 & 15 \end{bmatrix}$

$$\text{A} \square \square \text{C} \ominus \begin{bmatrix} \square & 1 \\ -1 & 1 \square \\ 1 & 15 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \square & \square & -\square \\ \square & \square & -\square & 1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square + \square & \square + \square & 6 - \square & -\square + 1 \\ -1 + \square 6 & -\square + \square & -\square - \square 6 & \square + 1 \square \\ 1 + \square \square & \square + \square & \square - \square \square & -\square + 15 \end{bmatrix}$$

$$\square \begin{bmatrix} \\ \square & \square & \square & -\square \\ \\ \square 5 & -\square & -\square & \square \\ \\ \square 1 & \square & -\square & 11 \\ \end{bmatrix}$$

$$\square \\ \begin{bmatrix} \\ \square & \square & -\square & -1 \\ \\ \square & \square & -\square & \square \\ \\ \square & -\square & -11 & \square \\ \end{bmatrix}$$

$$\begin{array}{c} \\ \text{other} \quad \text{AC} \quad \begin{bmatrix} \square & 6 & \square \\ -6 & \square & \square \\ \square & -\square & \square \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \square \\ -\square \\ \square \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square - 1 \square + \square 1 \\ -1 \square + \square + \square \\ 1 \square + 16 + \square \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square \\ 1 \square \\ \square \end{bmatrix} \\ \end{array}$$

and
$$\square \mathbf{C} \ \square \begin{bmatrix} \square & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & \square & \square \\ 1 & \square & \square \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \square \\ -\square \\ \square \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \square - \square + \square \\ \square + \square + 6 \\ \square - \square + \square \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ \square \\ -\square \end{bmatrix}$$

So $\quad \text{AC} \quad \square \square \text{C} \quad \square \begin{bmatrix} \square \\ 1 \square \\ \square \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ \square \\ -0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \square \\ \square \\ \square \end{bmatrix}$

Clearly, $\square \text{A} \square \square \text{C} \square \text{AC} \square \text{C}$

$$\square \begin{bmatrix} 6 & \square & 6 \\ \square & -6 & \square \\ \square & \square & 6 \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} -\square & -\square & -6 \\ -6 & \square & -6 \\ -\square & -\square & -\square \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} -\square & \square & \square \\ \square & -\square & \square \\ \square & \square & -\square \end{bmatrix}$$

$$\square \begin{bmatrix} \square & \square & \square \\ \square & \square & \square \\ \square & \square & \square \end{bmatrix} = \square$$
