First consider the graph (Fig $\square$1) to the right of the origin. $\square$bserve that as we
move from left to right along the graph, the height of the graph continuously increases.
For this reason, the function is said to be increasing for the real numbers $x$ $\square$0.

$\square$ow consider the graph to the left of the origin and observe here that as we move
from left to right along the graph, the height of the graph continuously decreases.
Consequently, the function is said to be decreasing for the real numbers $x$ $\square 0$.

We shall now give the following analytical definitions for a function which is
increasing or decreasing on an interval.

Definition 1 $\square$et I be an interval contained in the domain of a real valued function $f$.
Then $f$ is said to be

(i) increasing on I if $x_1 \square x_{\square}$ in I $\Rightarrow f(x_1) \leq f(x_{\square})$ for all $x_1, x_{\square} \in$ I.
(ii) strictly increasing on I if $x_1 \square x_{\square}$ in I $\Rightarrow f(x_1) \square f(x_{\square})$ for all $x_1, x_{\square} \in$ I.

<!-- page 215 -->
(iii) decreasing on I if $x_1 \square x_{\square}$ in I $\Rightarrow f(x_1) \geq f(x_{\square})$ for all $x_1, x_{\square} \in$ I.
(iv) strictly decreasing on I if $x_1 \square x_{\square}$ in I $\Rightarrow f(x_1) \square f(x_{\square})$ for all $x_1, x_{\square} \in$ I.

For graphical representation of such functions see Fig $\square\square$

Strictly Decreasing function
(iv)

We shall now define when a function is increasing or decreasing at a point.

Definition 2 $\square$et $x_0$ be a point in the domain of definition of a real valued function $f$.
Then $f$ is said to be increasing, strictly increasing, decreasing or strictly decreasing at
$x_0$ if there exists an open interval I containing $x_0$ such that $f$ is increasing, strictly
increasing, decreasing or strictly decreasing, respectively, in I.

$\square$et us clarify this definition for the case of increasing function.

A function $f$ is said to be increasing at $x_0$ if there exists an interval I = $(x_0 \square h, x_0 \square h)$,
$h \square 0$ such that for $x_1, x_{\square} \in \text{I}$

$$x_1 \square x_{\boxminus} \text{ in } I \Rightarrow f(x_1) \leq f(x_{\boxminus})$$
Similarly, the other cases can be clarified.

Example 7 Show that the function given by $f(x) = \text{x} \text{ x } \text{x}$ is strictly increasing on $\mathbf{R}$.

Solution $\square$et $x_1$ and $x_\square$ be any two numbers in $\mathbf{R}$. Then
$$x_1 \square x_\square \Rightarrow \bar{x}_1 \square \bar{x}_\square \Rightarrow \bar{x}_1 \square \square \square \bar{x}_\square \square \square \Rightarrow f(x_1) \square f(x_\square)$$

<!-- page 216 -->
$\square 01$

Thus, by $\square$ definition 1, it follows that $f$ is strictly increasing on $\mathbf{R}$.

We shall now give the first derivative test for increasing and decreasing functions.
The proof of this test requires the Mean $\square$alue Theorem studied in Chapter 5.

Theorem 1 $\square$et $f$ be continuous on $\square a$, $b \square$and differentiable on the open interval
$(a,b)$. Then

(a) $f$ is strictly increasing in $\square a,b \square$if $f'(x) \square 0$ for each $x \in (a, b)$

(b) $f$ is strictly decreasing in $\square a,b \square$if $f'(x) \square 0$ for each $x \in (a, b)$

(c) $f$ is a constant function in $\square a,b \square$if $f'(x) = 0$ for each $x \in (a, b)$

Proof (a) $\Box$et $x_1, x_{\Box} \in \Box a, b \Box$be such that $x_1 \Box x_{\Box}$.

Then, by Mean $\square$alue Theorem (Theorem $\square$in Chapter 5), there exists a point $c$
between $x_1$ and $x_\square$ such that

$$f(x_{\square}) \quad \square f(x_1) = f'(c) \quad (x_{\square} \quad \square x_1)$$
i.e.
$$f(x_{\square}) \quad \square f(x_1) \quad \square 0 \quad \text{(as } f'(c) \quad \square 0 \text{ (given)})$$
i.e.
$$f(x_{\square}) \quad \square f(x_1)$$
Thus, we have

Thus, we have

$$x_1 < x_{\square} \Rightarrow f(x_1) < f(x_{\square}), \text{ for all } x_1, x_{\square} \in \square a, b \square$$

Hence, $f$ is an increasing function in $\square a, b \square$

The proofs of part (b) and (c) are similar. It is left as an exercise to the reader.

Remarks

(i) There is a more generalised theorem, which states that if $f'(x) \square 0$ for $x$ in an
interval excluding the end points and $f$ is continuous in the interval, then $f$ is
strictly increasing. Similarly, if $f'(x) \square 0$ for $x$ in an interval excluding the end
points and $f$ is continuous in the interval, then $f$ is strictly decreasing.
(ii) If a function is strictly increasing or strictly decreasing in an interval I, then it is
necessarily increasing or decreasing in I. However, the converse need not
be true.

Example 8 Show that the function $f$ given by
$$f(x) = x^\square \square \square x^\square \square 4x, x \in \mathbf{R}$$
is strictly increasing on $\mathbf{R}$.
Solution $\square$ote that
$$f'(x) = \square x^\square \square \square x \square 4$$

<!-- page 217 -->
$$= \boxminus (x^\square \boxminus \boxminus x \square 1) \square 1$$
$$= \boxminus (x \square 1)^\square \square 1 \square 0, \text{ in every interval of } \mathbf{R}$$

Therefore, the function $f$ is strictly increasing on $\mathbf{R}$.

Example 9 $\square$rove that the function given by $f(x) = \cos x$ is

(a) strictly decreasing in $(0, \pi)$

(b) strictly increasing in $(\pi, \square\pi)$, and

(c) neither increasing nor decreasing in $(0, \square\pi)$.

Solution $\square$ote that $f'(x) = \square\sin x$

(a) Since for each $x \in (0, \pi)$, $\sin x \square 0$, we have $f'(x) \square 0$ and so $f$ is strictly
decreasing in $(0, \pi)$.

(b) Since for each $x \in (\pi, \square\pi)$, $\sin x \square 0$, we have $f'(x) \square 0$ and so $f$ is strictly
increasing in $(\pi, \square\pi)$.

(c) Clearly by (a) and (b) above, $f$ is neither increasing nor decreasing in $(0, \square\pi)$.

Example 10 Find the intervals in which the function $f$ given by $f(x) = x^{\square} \square 4x \square \square$ is
(a) strictly increasing (b) strictly decreasing

Solution We have
$$f(x) = x^{\square} \square 4x \square \square$$
or $$f'(x) = \square x \square 4$$

Therefore, $f'(x) = 0$ gives $x = \square \square$ow
the point $x = \square$ divides the real line into two
disjoint intervals namely, $(\square \infty, \square)$ and $(\square,$
$\infty)$ (Fig $\square \square$). In the interval $(\square \infty, \square), f'(x)$
$= \square x \square 4 \square 0$.

Therefore, $f$ is strictly decreasing in this interval. Also, in the interval $(\square \infty)$,
$f'(x) > 0$ and so the function $f$ is strictly increasing in this interval.

Example 11 Find the intervals in which the function $f$ given by $f(x) = 4x^{\square} \square \square x^{\square} \square \square \square x \square \square 0$
is (a) strictly increasing (b) strictly decreasing.

Solution We have
$$f(x) = 4x^{\square} \square \square x^{\square} \square \square \square x \square \square 0$$

<!-- page 218 -->
or
$$f'(x) = 1 \square x^0 \square 1 \square x \square \square \square \\ = 1 \square (x^0 \square x \square \square) \\ = 1 \square (x \square \square) (x \square \square)$$

Therefore, $f'(x) = 0$ gives $x = \square\square$, $\square$ The
points $x = \square\square$and $x = \square$ divides the real line into
three disjoint intervals, namely, $(\square\infty, \square\square), (\square\square, \square)$
and $(\square, \infty)$.

Fig 6.4

In the intervals $(\square \infty, \square \square)$ and $(\square, \infty), f'(x)$ is positive while in the interval $(\square \square, \square)$,
$f'(x)$ is negative. Consequently, the function $f$ is strictly increasing in the intervals
$(\square \infty, \square \square)$ and $(\square, \infty)$ while the function is strictly decreasing in the interval $(\square \square, \square)$.
However, $f$ is neither increasing nor decreasing in $\mathbf{R}$.

Example 12 Find intervals in which the function given by $f(x) = \sin \square x$, $x \in \left[ 0, \frac{\pi}{\square} \right]$ is
(a) increasing (b) decreasing.

Solution We have

uation We have
$$f(x) = \sin \square x$$
or
$$f'(x) = \square \cos \square x$$

Therefore, $f'(x) = 0$ gives $\cos \square x = 0$ which in turn gives $\square x = \frac{\pi}{\square}, \frac{\square \pi}{\square}$ (as $x \in \left[ 0, \frac{\pi}{\square} \right]$

implies $\square x \in \left[ 0, \frac{\square \pi}{\square} \right]$. So $x = \frac{\pi}{\square}$ and $\frac{\pi}{\square}$ . The point $x = \frac{\pi}{\square}$ divides the interval $\left[ 0, \frac{\pi}{\square} \right]$

into two disjoint intervals $\left[ 0, \frac{\pi}{\square} \right)$ and $\left( \frac{\pi}{\square}, \frac{\pi}{\square} \right)$.

Fig 6.5

Fig 6.5

$\square$ow, $f'(x) > 0$ for all $x \in \left[ 0, \frac{\pi}{\square} \right)$ as $0 \le x < \frac{\pi}{\square} \Rightarrow 0 \le \square x < \frac{\pi}{\square}$ and $f'(x) < 0$ for

all $x \in \left( \frac{\pi}{\square}, \frac{\pi}{\square} \right)$ as $\frac{\pi}{\square} < x < \frac{\pi}{\square} \Rightarrow \frac{\pi}{\square} < \square x < \frac{\square \pi}{\square}$.

Therefore, $f$ is strictly increasing in $\left[ 0, \frac{\pi}{\square} \right)$ and strictly decreasing in $\left( \frac{\pi}{\square}, \frac{\pi}{\square} \right)$.

Also, the given function is continuous at $x=0$ and $x=\frac{\pi}{\epsilon}$. Therefore, by Theorem 1,

$$f \text{ is increasing on } \left[ 0, \frac{\pi}{\square} \right] \text{ and decreasing on } \left[ \frac{\pi}{\square}, \frac{\pi}{\square} \right].$$

Example 13 Find the intervals in which the function $f$ given by
$$f(x) = \sin x \quad \square \cos x, 0 \le x \le \square \pi$$
is strictly increasing or strictly decreasing.

Solution We have
$$f(x) = \sin x \quad \square \cos x,$$
or
$$f'(x) = \cos x \quad \square \sin x$$

$\square$ow $f'(x) = 0$ gives $\sin x = \cos x$ which gives that $x = \frac{\pi}{4}, \frac{5\pi}{4}$ as $0 \le x \le \square \pi$

The points $x = \frac{\pi}{4}$ and $x = \frac{5\pi}{4}$ divide the interval $[0, \pi]$ into three disjoint intervals,
namely, $\left[ 0, \frac{\pi}{4} \right), \left( \frac{\pi}{4}, \frac{5\pi}{4} \right)$ and $\left( \frac{5\pi}{4}, \square \pi \right]$.

or $f$ is strictly increasing in the intervals $\left[ 0, \frac{\pi}{4} \right)$ and $\left( \frac{5\pi}{4}, \square \pi \right]$

Also $f'(x) < 0$ if $x \in \left( \frac{\pi}{4}, \frac{5\pi}{4} \right)$

or $f$ is strictly decreasing in $\left( \frac{\pi}{4}, \frac{5\pi}{4} \right)$

6.4 Tangents and Normals
In this section, we shall use differentiation to find the equation of the tangent line and
the normal line to a curve at a given point.
Recall that the equation of a straight line passing through a given point $(x_0, y_0)$
having finite slope $m$ is given by

$$y \square y_0 = m(x \square x_0)$$

<!-- page 222 -->
$\square$ote that the slope of the tangent to the curve $y=f(x)$


at the point $(x_0, y_0)$ is given by $\left.\frac{dy}{dx}\right}_{(x_0, y_0)}$ $(=f'(x_0))$. So


the equation of the tangent at $(x_0, y_0)$ to the curve $y=f(x)$
is given by

Fig 6.7

$$y \square y_0 = f'(x_0)(x \square x_0)$$

Also, since the normal is perpendicular to the tangent,
the slope of the normal to the curve $y = f(x)$ at $(x_0, y_0)$ is
$\frac{-1}{f'(x_0)}$, if $f'(x_0) \neq 0$. Therefore, the equation of the
normal to the curve $y = f(x)$ at $(x_0, y_0)$ is given by

$$y \square y_0 = \frac{-1}{f'(x_0)} (x - x_0)$$

i.e. $$(y-y_0)f'(x_0) + (x-x_0) = 0$$

Note If a tangent line to the curve $y = f(x)$ ma es an angle $\theta$ with $x$ axis in the
positive direction, then $\frac{dy}{dx} =$ slope of the tangent $= \tan \theta$ .

Particular cases

(i) If slope of the tangent line is $\square$ero, then $\tan \theta = 0$ and so $\theta = 0$ which means the
tangent line is parallel to the $x\overline{\text{ axis}}$. In this case, the equation of the tangent at
the point $(x_0, y_0)$ is given by $y = y_0$.


(ii) If $\theta \rightarrow \frac{\pi}{\square}$, then $\tan \theta \rightarrow \infty$, which means the tangent line is perpendicular to the
$x\overline{\text{ axis}}$, i.e., parallel to the $y\overline{\text{ axis}}$. In this case, the equation of the tangent at
$(x_0, y_0)$ is given by $x = x_0$ (Why $\square$).

Example 14 Find the slope of the tangent to the curve $y = x^{\square} \square x$ at $x = \square$

Solution The slope of the tangent at $x = \square$ is given by

$$\left. \frac{dy}{dx} \right]_{x=\square} = \square x^{\square} - 1 \right]_{x=\square} = 11.$$

Example 15 Find the point at which the tangent to the curve $y = \sqrt{4x - \square} - 1$ has its

slope $\frac{\square}{\square}$.

Example 15 Find the point at which the tangent to the curve $y = \sqrt{4x - \square} - 1$ has its

slope $\frac{c}{\square}$.

Solution Slope of tangent to the given curve at $(x, y)$ is

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\square} (4x - \square)^{-1} \square 4 = \frac{\square}{\sqrt{4x - \square}}$$

The slope is given to be $\frac{c}{\square}$.

So $\frac{\square}{\sqrt{4x - c}} = \frac{c}{\square}$

or $4x \square \square = 9$

or $x = \square$

$\square$ow $y = \sqrt{4x - \square} - 1$. So when $x = \square$, $y = \sqrt{4(\square) - \square} - 1 = c$.

Therefore, the required point is $(\square, \square)$.

Example 16 Find the equation of all lines having slope $\square$and being tangent to the curve

$y + \frac{\square}{x - c} = 0$.

Solution Slope of the tangent to the given curve at any point $(x, y)$ is given by

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{\square}{(x - \square)^2}$$

$\square$ut the slope is given to be $\square$ Therefore

$$\frac{\square}{(x - \square)^2} = \square$$

or $(x \square \square)^2 = 1$

or $x \square \square = \square 1$

or $x = \square, 4$

$\square$ow $x = \square$gives $y = \square$and $x = 4$ gives $y = \square \square$. Thus, there are two tangents to the

given curve with slope $\square$and passing through the points $(\square, \square)$ and $(4, \square \square)$. The equation

of tangent through $(\square, \square)$ is given by

$$y \square \square = \square (x \square \square)$$

or $y \square \square x \square \square = 0$

and the equation of the tangent through $(4, \square \square)$ is given by

$$y \square (\square \square) = \square (x \square 4)$$

or $y \square \square x \square 10 = 0$

Example 17 Find points on the curve $\frac{x^{\square}}{4} + \frac{y^{\square}}{\square 5} = 1$ at which the tangents are (i) parallel
to $x \overline{\text{a}}$xis (ii) parallel to $y \overline{\text{a}}$xis.

Solution $\square$ifferentiating $\frac{x^{\square}}{4} + \frac{y^{\square}}{5} = 1$ with respect to $x$, we get

$$\frac{x}{\square} + \frac{\square y}{\square 5} \frac{dy}{dx} = 0$$

or $$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{-\square 5}{4} \frac{x}{y}$$

(i) $\square$ow, the tangent is parallel to the $x$ axis if the slope of the tangent is $\square$ero which

gives $\frac{-\square 5}{4} \frac{x}{y} = 0$. This is possible if $x = 0$. Then $\frac{x^{\square}}{4} + \frac{y^{\square}}{\square 5} = 1$ for $x = 0$ gives

$y^{\square} = \square 5$, i.e., $y = \square 5$.

Thus, the points at which the tangents are parallel to the $x$ axis are $(0, 5)$ and

$(0, \square 5)$.

(ii) The tangent line is parallel to $y$ axis if the slope of the normal is $0$ which gives

$\frac{4y}{\square 5x} = 0$, i.e., $y = 0$. Therefore, $\frac{x^{\square}}{4} + \frac{y^{\square}}{\square 5} = 1$ for $y = 0$ gives $x = \square \square$ Hence, the

points at which the tangents are parallel to the $y$ axis are $(\square, 0)$ and $(\square, 0)$.

Example 18 Find the equation of the tangent to the curve $y = \frac{x - \square}{(x - \square)(x - \square)}$ at the
point where it cuts the $x\square$ axis.

Solution $\square$ote that on $x$ axis, $y = 0$. So the equation of the curve, when $y = 0$, gives
$x = \square$ Thus, the curve cuts the $x$ axis at $(\square, 0)$. $\square$ow differentiating the equation of the
curve with respect to $x$, we obtain

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1 - y(\square x - 5)}{(x - \square)(x - \square)} \qquad (\text{Why}\square)$$

or $$\left.\frac{dy}{dx}\right]_{(\Box,0)} = \frac{1-0}{(5)(4)} = \frac{1}{\Box 0}$$

<!-- page 225 -->
Therefore, the slope of the tangent at $(\square, 0)$ is $\frac{1}{\square 0}$. Hence, the equation of the
tangent at $(\square, 0)$ is

$$y - 0 = \frac{1}{\square 0}(x - \square) \quad \text{or} \quad \square 0y - x + \square = 0$$

Example 19 Find the equations of the tangent and normal to the curve $x^{\square} + y^{\square} = \square$
at $(1, 1)$.

at $(1, 1)$.


Solution $\square$ differentiating $x^{\square} + y^{\square} = \square$ with respect to $x$, we get


$$\frac{\square}{x} \frac{-1}{\square} + \frac{\square}{x} y \frac{-1}{\square} \frac{dy}{dx} = 0$$


or


$$\frac{dy}{dx} = -\left( \frac{y}{x} \right)^{\frac{1}{\square}}$$


Therefore, the slope of the tangent at $(1, 1)$ is $\left. \frac{dy}{dx} \right]_{(1, 1)} = -1$.


So the equation of the tangent at $(1, 1)$ is


$$y \square 1 = \square 1 \ (x \square 1) \quad \text{or} \quad y \square x \square \square = 0$$


Also, the slope of the normal at $(1, 1)$ is given by


$$\frac{-1}{\text{slope of the tangent at } (1, 1)} = 1$$


Therefore, the equation of the normal at $(1, 1)$ is


$$y \square 1 = 1 \ (x \square 1) \quad \text{or} \quad y \square x = 0$$


Example 20 Find the equation of tangent to the curve given by


$$x = a \sin \square t, \quad y = b \cos \square t \quad \dots (1)$$


at a point where $t = \frac{\pi}{\square}$.


Solution $\square$ differentiating $(1)$ with respect to $t$, we get


$$\frac{dx}{dt} = \square a \sin \square t \cos t \quad \text{and} \quad \frac{dy}{dt} = -\square b \cos \square t \sin t$$

<!-- page 226 -->
A□□□ICATI□□ ☐F ☐ERI□ATI□ES

$\square 11$

or $$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{\frac{dy}{dt}}{\frac{dx}{dt}} = \frac{-\square b \cos \square t \sin t}{\square a \sin \square t \cos t} = \frac{-b}{a} \frac{\cos t}{\sin t}$$

Therefore, slope of the tangent at $t = \frac{\pi}{\square}$ is

$$\left. \frac{dy}{dx} \right]_{t=\frac{\pi}{\square}} = \frac{-b \cos \frac{\pi}{\square}}{a \sin \frac{\pi}{\square}} = 0$$

Also, when $t = \frac{\pi}{\square}$, $x = a$ and $y = 0$. Hence, the equation of tangent to the given

curve at $t = \frac{\pi}{\square}$, i.e., at $(a, 0)$ is

$$y \square 0 = 0(x \square a), \text{ i.e., } y = 0.$$

$$y \square 0 = 0(x \square a), \text{ i.e., } y = 0.$$

6.5 Approximations

In this section, we will use differentials to approximate values of certain quantities.

$\Box$et $f \Box \Box \rightarrow \mathbf{R}$, $\Box \subset \mathbf{R}$, be a given function
and let $y = f(x)$. $\Box$et $\Delta x$ denote a small
increment in $x$. Recall that the increment in $y$
corresponding to the increment in $x$, denoted
by $\Delta y$, is given by $\Delta y = f(x \Box \Delta x) \Box f(x)$. We
define the following

(i) The differential of $x$, denoted by $dx$, is
defined by $dx = \Delta x$.

(ii) The differential of $y$, denoted by $dy$,
is defined by $dy = f'(x) \ dx$ or

$$dy = \left( \frac{dy}{dx} \right) \Delta x.$$

Fig 6.8

$\square$ Two curves intersect at right angle if the tangents to the curves at the point of intersection
are perpendicular to each other.

<!-- page 229 -->
In case $dx = \Delta x$ is relatively small when compared with $x$, $dy$ is a good approximation
of $\Delta y$ and we denote it by $dy \approx \Delta y$.

For geometrical meaning of $\Delta x, \Delta y, \ dx$ and $dy$, one may refer to Fig $\square\square$

Note In view of the above discussion and Fig $\square\square$, we may note that the
differential of the dependent variable is not equal to the increment of the variable
where as the differential of independent variable is equal to the increment of the
variable.

Example 21 $\square$se differential to approximate $\sqrt{\square\square}$.

Example 21 $\square$se differential to approximate $\sqrt{\square\square}$.

Solution Ta$\square$e $y = \sqrt{x}$. $\square$et $x = \square\square$and let $\Delta x = 0.\square$ Then

$$\Delta y = \sqrt{x + \Delta x} - \sqrt{x} = \sqrt{\square\square\square} - \sqrt{\square\square} = \sqrt{\square\square\square} - \square$$

or $\sqrt{\square\square\square} = \square\square\square \Delta y$

$\square$ow $dy$ is approximately equal to $\Delta y$ and is given by

$$dy = \left( \frac{dy}{dx} \right) \Delta x = \frac{1}{\square\sqrt{x}} (0.\square) = \frac{1}{\square\sqrt{\square\square}} (0.\square) = 0.05 \qquad (\text{as } y = \sqrt{x})$$

Thus, the approximate value of $\sqrt{\square\square\square}$ is $\square\square\square 0.05 = \square\square 05$.

Example 22 $\square$se differential to approximate $(\square 5)^{\frac{1}{\square}}$.

Solution $\square$et $y = x^{\frac{1}{\square}}$. $\square$et $x = \square\square$and let $\Delta x = \square\square\square$ Then

$$\Delta y = (x + \Delta x)^{\frac{1}{\square}} - x^{\frac{1}{\square}} = (\square 5)^{\frac{1}{\square}} - (\square\square)^{\frac{1}{\square}} = (\square 5)^{\frac{1}{\square}} - \square$$

or $(\square 5)^{\frac{1}{\square}} = \square\square\square \Delta y$

$\square$ow $dy$ is approximately equal to $\Delta y$ and is given by

$$dy = \left( \frac{dy}{dx} \right) \Delta x = \frac{1}{\square x^{\frac{\square}{\square}}} (-\square) \qquad (\text{as } y = x^{\frac{1}{\square}})$$

$$= \frac{1}{\square \left( (\square\square)^{\frac{1}{\square}} \right)^{\square}} (-\square) = \frac{-\square}{\square\square} = -0.0\square 4$$

Thus, the approximate value of $(\square 5)^{\frac{1}{\square}}$ is given by

$$\square\square (\square 0.0\square 4) = \square 9\square\square$$

<!-- page 230 -->
Example 23 Find the approximate value of $f(\square 0 \square)$, where $f(x) = \square x^0 \square 5x \square \square$

Example 23 Find the approximate value of $f(\square 0\square)$, where $f(x) = \square x^0 \square 5x \square \square$

Solution $\square$et $x = \square$and $\Delta x = 0.0 \square$ Then

$$f(\square \ 0\square) = f(x \square \Delta x) = \square (x \square \Delta x)^0 \square 5(x \square \Delta x) \square \square$$

$\square$ote that $\Delta y = f(x \square \Delta x) \square f(x)$. Therefore

$$f(x \square \Delta x) = f(x) \square \Delta y$$

$$\approx f(x) \square f'(x) \Delta x \quad \text{(as } dx = \Delta x \text{)}$$

or

$$f(\square 0\square) \approx (\square x^0 \square 5x \square \square) \square (\square (x \square 5) \Delta x$$

$$= (\square (\square)^0 \square 5(\square \square \square) \square (\square (\square \square) \square 5)(0.0\square) \quad \text{(as } x = \square, \Delta x = 0.0\square \text{)}$$

$$= (\square \square \square 15 \square \square) \square (1 \square \square 5)(0.0\square)$$

$$= 45 \square 0.4 \square = 45.4 \square$$

Hence, approximate value of $f(\square 0 \square)$ is $45.4 \square$

Example 24 Find the approximate change in the volume $\square$ of a cube of side $x$ meters
caused by increasing the side by $\square$.

Solution $\square$ote that

$$\square = x^{\square}$$

or

$$d \square = \left( \frac{d \square}{dx} \right) \Delta x = (\square x^{\square}) \Delta x$$

$$= (\square x^{\square}) (0.0 \square x) = 0.0 \square x^{\square} m^{\square} \quad \text{(as } \square \text{ of } x \text{ is } 0.0 \square x \text{)}$$

Thus, the approximate change in volume is $0.0 \square x^{\square} m^{\square}$.

Thus, the approximate change in volume is $0.0 \square x^2 m^2$.

Example 25 If the radius of a sphere is measured as 9 cm with an error of $0.0\square\text{cm}$,
then find the approximate error in calculating its volume.

Solution $\square$et $r$ be the radius of the sphere and $\Delta r$ be the error in measuring the radius.
Then $r = 9\text{ cm}$ and $\Delta r = 0.0\square\text{cm}$. $\square$ow, the volume $\square$ of the sphere is given by

$$\square = \frac{4}{\square}\pi r^{\square}$$

or $$\frac{d\square}{dr} = 4\pi r^{\square}$$

Therefore $$\begin{aligned} d\square &= \left(\frac{d\square}{dr}\right)\Delta r = (4\pi r^{\square})\Delta r \\ &= 4\pi(9)^{\square}(0.0\square) = 9.\square\pi\text{cm}^{\square} \end{aligned}$$

Thus, the approximate error in calculating the volume is 9.$\square\pi\text{cm}^{\square}$.

Thus, the approximate error in calculating the volume is 9. $\pi \text{cm}^3$.

6.6 Maxima and Minima

In this section, we will use the concept of derivatives to calculate the maximum or
minimum values of various functions. In fact, we will find the turning points of the
graph of a function and thus find points at which the graph reaches its highest (or

<!-- page 232 -->
A□□□ICATI□□ ☐F ☐ERI□ATI□ES

$\square 1$ $\square$

lowest) $locally$. The $\square$nowledge of such points is very useful in $s\square$etching the graph of
a given function. Further, we will also find the absolute maximum and absolute minimum
of a function that are necessary for the solution of many applied problems.

$\square$et us consider the following problems that arise in day to day life.

(i) The profit from a grove of orange trees is given by $\square(x) = ax \square bx^{\square}$, where $a,b$
are constants and $x$ is the number of orange trees per acre. How many trees per
acre will maximise the profit $\square$

(ii) A ball, thrown into the air from a building $\square 0$ metres high, travels along a path

given by $h(x) = \square 0 + x - \frac{x^{\square}}{\square 0}$, where $x$ is the horizontal distance from the building

and $h(x)$ is the height of the ball . What is the maximum height the ball will
reach $\square$

(iii) An Apache helicopter of enemy is flying along the path given by the curve
$f(x) = x^{\square} \square \square$ A soldier, placed at the point $(1, \square)$, wants to shoot the helicopter
when it is nearest to him. What is the nearest distance $\square$

In each of the above problem, there is something common, i.e., we wish to find out
the maximum or minimum values of the given functions. In order to tac $\bar{1}$e such problems,
we first formally define maximum or minimum values of a function, points of local
maxima and minima and test for determining such points.

Definition 3 $\square$et $f$ be a function defined on an interval I. Then

(a) $f$ is said to have a $maximum$ $value$ in I, if there exists a point $c$ in I such that
$f(c) > f(x)$, for all $x \in \text{I}$.
The number $f(c)$ is called the maximum value of $f$ in I and the point $c$ is called a
$point$ $of$ $maximum$ $value$ of $f$ in I.
(b) $f$ is said to have a minimum value in I, if there exists a point $c$ in I such that
$f(c) < f(x)$, for all $x \in \text{I}$.
The number $f(c)$, in this case, is called the minimum value of $f$ in I and the point
$c$, in this case, is called a $point$ $of$ $minimum$ $value$ of $f$ in I.
(c) $f$ is said to have an extreme value in I if there exists a point $c$ in I such that
$f(c)$ is either a maximum value or a minimum value of $f$ in I.

The number $f(c)$, in this case, is called an $extreme$ $value$ of $f$ in I and the point $c$
is called an $extreme$ $point$.

$\mathit{Remark}$ In Fig $\square$9(a), (b) and (c), we have exhibited that graphs of certain particular
functions help us to find maximum value and minimum value at a point. Infact, through
graphs, we can even find maximum minimum value of a function at a point at which it
is not even differentiable (Example $\square$).

Example 26 Find the maximum and the minimum values,
if any, of the function $f$ given by
$$f(x) = x^{\square}, x \in \mathbf{R}.$$
Solution From the graph of the given function (Fig $\square$10),
we have $f(x) = 0$ if $x = 0$. Also
$$f(x) \geq 0, \text{ for all } x \in \mathbf{R}.$$
Therefore, the minimum value of $f$ is 0 and the point
of minimum value of $f$ is $x = 0$. Further, it may be observed
from the graph of the function that $f$ has no maximum
value and hence no point of maximum value of $f$ in $\mathbf{R}$.

Fig 6.10

Note If we restrict the domain of $f$ to $\square\square$, $1\square$ only,
then $f$ will have maximum value $(\square\square)^n = 4$ at $x = \square\square$

of $f$, if any, of the function given by $f(x) = \text{x} \text{ , } x \in \mathbf{R}$.

Solution From the graph of the given function
(Fig $\square$ 11) , note that

$f(x) \geq 0$, for all $x \in \mathbf{R}$ and $f(x) = 0$ if $x = 0$.

Therefore, the function $f$ has a minimum value $0$
and the point of minimum value of $f$ is $x = 0$. Also, the
graph clearly shows that $f$ has no maximum value in
$\mathbf{R}$ and hence no point of maximum value in $\mathbf{R}$.

Fig 6.11

Note

(i) If we restrict the domain of $f$ to $\boxplus \square$, $1 \square$only, then $f$ will have maximum value
$\boxplus \square \boxplus \mp \square$

Example 28 Find the maximum and the minimum values, if any, of the function
given by

$$f(x) = x, x \in (0, 1).$$

Solution The given function is an increasing (strictly) function in the given interval
$(0, 1)$. From the graph (Fig $\square 1 \square$) of the function $f$, it
seems that, it should have the minimum value at a
point closest to $0$ on its right and the maximum value
at a point closest to $1$ on its left. Are such points
available $\square \square$ f course, not. It is not possible to locate
such points. Infact, if a point $x_0$ is closest to $0$, then

$$\Downarrow_{Y'} f(x) = x \text{ in } (0,1)$$

Fig 6.12

we find $\frac{x_0}{\square} < x_0$ for all $x_0 \in (0,1)$. Also, if $x_1$ is $\mathbf{X} \leftarrow \text{O}$

closest to 1, then $\frac{x_1 + 1}{[} > x_1$ for all $x_1 \in (0,1)$.

Therefore, the given function has neither the maximum value nor the minimum
value in the interval $(0,1)$.

$\mathit{Remark}$ The reader may observe that in Example $\boxdot$, if we include the points $0$ and $1$
in the domain of $f$, i.e., if we extend the domain of $f$ to $\boxdot, 1 \boxdot$ then the function $f$ has
minimum value $0$ at $x = 0$ and maximum value $1$ at $x = 1$. Infact, we have the following
results (The proof of these results are beyond the scope of the present text)

Every monotonic function assumes its maximum/minimum value at the end
points of the domain of definition of the function.

A more general result is

Every continuous function on a closed interval has a maximum and a minimum
value.

Note $\square$y a monotonic function $f$ in an interval I, we mean that $f$ is either
increasing in I or decreasing in I.

Maximum and minimum values of a function defined on a closed interval will be
discussed later in this section.

$\square$et us now examine the graph of a function as shown in Fig $\square 1$ $\square$ $\square$bserve that at
points A, $\square$, C and $\square$ on the graph, the function changes its nature from decreasing to
increasing or vice $\square$versa. These points may be called *turning points* of the given
function. Further, observe that at turning points, the graph has either a little hill or a little
valley. Roughly spea$\square$ing, the function has minimum value in some neighbourhood
(interval) of each of the points A and C which are at the bottom of their respective

<!-- page 235 -->
Fig 6.13

valleys. Similarly, the function has maximum value in some neighbourhood of points $\square$
and $\square$ which are at the top of their respective hills. For this reason, the points A and C
may be regarded as points of $local$ $minimum$ $value$ (or $relative$ $minimum$ $value$) and
points $\square$ and $\square$ may be regarded as points of $local$ $maximum$ $value$ (or $relative$ $maximum$
$value$) for the function. The $local$ $maximum$ $value$ and $local$ $minimum$ $value$ of the
function are referred to as $local$ $maxima$ and $local$ $minima$, respectively, of the function.
We now formally give the following definition

We now formally give the following definition

Definition 4 $\square$et $f$ be a real valued function and let $c$ be an interior point in the domain
of $f$. Then

(a) $c$ is called a point of $local$ $maxima$ if there is an $h$ $\square 0$ such that
$f(c) > f(x)$, for all $x$ in $(c$ $\square h$, $c$ $\square h)$
The value $f(c)$ is called the $local$ $maximum$ $value$ of $f$.
(b) $c$ is called a point of $local$ $minima$ if there is an $h$ $\square 0$ such that
$f(c) < f(x)$, for all $x$ in $(c$ $\square h$, $c$ $\square h)$

The value $f(\text{c})$ is called the \textit{local minimum value} of $f$.

$\square$eometrically, the above definition states that if $x = c$ is a point of local maxima of $f$,
then the graph of $f$ around $c$ will be as shown in Fig $\square 14$(a). $\square$ote that the function $f$ is
increasing (i.e., $f'(x) \square 0$) in the interval $(c \square h, c)$ and decreasing (i.e., $f'(x) \square 0$) in the
interval $(c, c \square h)$.

This suggests that $f'(c)$ must be $\square$ero.

Fig 6.14

<!-- page 236 -->
Similarly, if $c$ is a point of local minima of $f$, then the graph of $f$ around $c$ will be as
shown in Fig $\square 14$(b). Here $f$ is decreasing (i.e., $f'(x) \square 0$) in the interval $(c \square h, c)$ and
increasing (i.e., $f'(x) \square 0$) in the interval $(c, c \square h)$. This again suggest that $f'(c)$ must
be $\square$ero.

The above discussion lead us to the following theorem (without proof).

**Theorem 2** $\square$et $f$ be a function defined on an open interval I. Suppose $c \in \text{I}$ be any
point. If $f$ has a local maxima or a local minima at $x = c$, then either $f'(c) = 0$ or $f$ is not
differentiable at $c$.
$\mathbf{Y}$

$\mathit{Remark}$ The converse of above theorem need
not be true, that is, a point at which the derivative
vanishes need not be a point of local maxima or
local minima. For example, if $f(x) = x^{\square}$, then $f'(x)$
$= \square x^{\square}$ and so $f'(0) = 0$. $\square$ut $0$ is neither a point of
local maxima nor a point of local minima (Fig $\square$15).

Fig 6.15

Note A point c in the domain of a function
$f$ at which either $f'(c) = 0$ or $f$ is not differentiable
is called a critical point of $f$. $\square$ote that if $f$ is
continuous at $c$ and $f'(c) = 0$, then there exists
an $h$ $\square 0$ such that $f$ is differentiable in the interval
$(c \square h, c \square h)$.

We shall now give a wor$\square$ing rule for finding points of local maxima or points of
local minima using only the first order derivatives.

**Theorem 3 (First Derivative Test)** $\square$et $f$ be a function defined on an open interval I.
$\square$et $f$ be continuous at a critical point $c$ in I. Then

(i) If $f'(x)$ changes sign from positive to negative as $x$ increases through $c$, i.e., if
$f'(x) \square 0$ at every point sufficiently close to and to the left of $c$, and $f'(x) \square 0$ at
every point sufficiently close to and to the right of $c$, then $c$ is a point of $local$
$maxima$.

(ii) If $f'(x)$ changes sign from negative to positive as $x$ increases through $c$, i.e., if
$f'(x) \square 0$ at every point sufficiently close to and to the left of $c$, and $f'(x) \square 0$ at
every point sufficiently close to and to the right of $c$, then $c$ is a point of $local$
$minima$.

(iii) If $f'(x)$ does not change sign as $x$ increases through $c$, then $c$ is neither a point of
local maxima nor a point of local minima. Infact, such a point is called $point$ $of$
$inflection$ (Fig $\square$15).

<!-- page 237 -->
$\square\square\square$ MATHEMATICS

Note If $c$ is a point of local maxima of $f$, then $f(c)$ is a local maximum value of
$f$. Similarly, if $c$ is a point of local minima of $f$, then $f(c)$ is a local minimum value of $f$.

Figures $\square 15$ and $\square 1$, geometrically explain Theorem $\square$

Example 29 Find all points of local maxima and local minima of the function $f$
given by

$$f(x) = x^{\square} \square \square x \square \square$$

Solution We have

$$f(x) = x^{\square} \square \square x \square \square$$

or $f'(x) = \square x^2 \square \square = \square (x \square 1) (x \square 1)$

or $f'(x) = 0$ at $x = 1$ and $x = \square 1$

Thus, $x = \square 1$ are the only critical points which could possibly be the points of local
maxima and $\bar{\text{or}}$ local minima of $f$. $\square$et us first examine the point $x = 1$.

$\square$ote that for values close to 1 and to the right of $1, f'(x) \square 0$ and for values close
to 1 and to the left of $1, f'(x) \square 0$. Therefore, by first derivative test, $x = 1$ is a point
of local minima and local minimum value is $f(1) = 1$. In the case of $x = \square$, note that
$f'(x) \square 0$, for values close to and to the left of $\square$ and $f'(x) \square 0$, for values close to and
to the right of $\square 1$. Therefore, by first derivative test, $x = \square 1$ is a point of local maxima
and local maximum value is $f(\square) = 5$.

Thus, $x = 1$ is the only critical point of $f$. We shall now examine this point for local
maxima and or local minima of $f$. $\square$bserve that $f'(x) \geq 0$, for all $x \in \mathbf{R}$ and in particular
$f'(x)$ $\square 0$, for values close to 1 and to the left and to the right of 1. Therefore, by first
derivative test, the point $x = 1$ is neither a point of local maxima nor a point of local
minima. Hence $x = 1$ is a point of inflexion.

$\mathit{Remark}$ $\square$ne may note that since $f'(x)$, in Example $\square 0$, never changes its sign on $\mathbf{R}$,
graph of $f$has no turning points and hence no point of local maxima or local minima.

We shall now give another test to examine local maxima and local minima of a
given function. This test is often easier to apply than the first derivative test.

Theorem 4 (Second Derivative Test) $\square$et $f$ be a function defined on an interval I
and $c \in \mathrm{I}$. $\square$et $f$ be twice differentiable at $c$. Then

(i) $x = c$ is a point of local maxima if $f'(c) = 0$ and $f''(c)$ $\square$ 0
The value $f(c)$ is local maximum value of $f$.

(ii) $x = c$ is a point of local minima if $f'(c) = 0$ and $f''(c)$ $\square$ 0
In this case, $f(c)$ is local minimum value of $f$.

(iii) The test fails if $f'(c) = 0$ and $f''(c) = 0$.

In this case, we go bac $\square$ to the first derivative test and find whether $c$ is a point of
local maxima, local minima or a point of inflexion.

Note As $f$ is twice differentiable at $c$, we mean
second order derivative of $f$ exists at $c$.

Example 31 Find local minimum value of the function $f$
given by $f(x) = \square \square \square \square, x \in \mathbf{R}$.

Solution $\square$ote that the given function is not differentiable
at $x = 0$. So, second derivative test fails. $\square$et us try first
derivative test. $\square$ote that $0$ is a critical point of $f$. $\square$ow
to the left of $0, f(x) = \square \square x$ and so $f'(x) = \square 1 \square 0$. Also

to the right of $0, f(x) = \square \square x$ and so $f'(x) = 1 \square 0$. Therefore, by first derivative test,
$x = 0$ is a point of local minima of $f$ and local minimum value of $f$ is $f(0) = \square$

Example 32 Find local maximum and local minimum values of the function $f$ given by

Example 32 Find local maximum and local minimum values of the function $f$ given by

$$f(x) = \square x^4 \quad \square 4x^2 \quad \square 1 \quad \square x^2 \quad \square 1 \quad \square$$

Solution We have

$$f(x) = \square x^4 \quad \square 4x^2 \quad \square 1 \quad \square x^2 \quad \square 1 \quad \square$$

or $$f'(x) = 1 \quad \square x^2 \quad \square 1 \quad \square x^2 \quad \square 4x = 1 \quad \square (x \quad \square 1) (x \quad \square \square)$$

or $$f'(x) = 0 \text{ at } x = 0, x = 1 \text{ and } x = \square \square$$

$\square \text{ow}$ $$f''(x) = \square \square x^2 \quad \square \square 4x \quad \square \square 4 = 1 \square (\square x^2 \quad \square \square x \quad \square 1)$$

$$or \quad \begin{cases} f''(0) &= -1 \square < 0 \\ f''(1) &= 4 \square > 0 \\ f''(-\square) = \square 4 > 0 \end{cases}$$

Therefore, by second derivative test, $x = 0$ is a point of local maxima and local
maximum value of $f$ at $x = 0$ is $f(0) = 1 \square$ while $x = 1$ and $x = \square \square$ are the points of local
minima and local minimum values of $f$ at $x = \square 1$ and $\square \square$ are $f(1) = \square$ and $f(\square) = \square \square 0$,
respectively.

Example 33 Find all the points of local maxima and local minima of the function $f$
given by

$$f(x) = \square x^0 \square \square x^0 \square \square x \square 5.$$

Solution We have

$$f(x) = \square x^0 \square \square x^0 \square \square x \square 5$$

or $$\begin{cases} f'(x) = \square x^{\square} - 1 \square x + \square = \square (x-1)^{\square} \\ f''(x) = 1 \square (x-1) \end{cases}$$

$\square$ow $f'(x) = 0$ gives $x = 1$. Also $f''(1) = 0$. Therefore, the second derivative test
fails in this case. So, we shall go bac $\square$ to the first derivative test.

We have already seen (Example $\square 0$) that, using first derivative test, $x=1$ is neither
a point of local maxima nor a point of local minima and so it is a point of inflexion.

Example 34 Find two positive numbers whose sum is 15 and the sum of whose
squares is minimum.

Solution $\square$et one of the numbers be $x$. Then the other number is $(15 \quad \square x)$. $\square$et $S(x)$
denote the sum of the squares of these numbers. Then

A□□□ICATI□□ ☐F ☐ERI□ATI□ES

$\square \square 5$

$$S(x) = x^{\square} \square (15 \square x)^{\square} = \square x^{\square} \square \square 0x \square \square 5$$

or $$\begin{cases} S'(x) = 4x - \square 0 \\ S''(x) = 4 \end{cases}$$

$\square$ow $S'(x) = 0$ gives $x = \frac{15}{\square}$. Also $S''\left(\frac{15}{\square}\right) = 4 > 0$. Therefore, by second derivative

test, $x = \frac{15}{\square}$ is the point of local minima of S. Hence the sum of squares of numbers is

minimum when the numbers are $\frac{15}{\square}$ and $15 - \frac{15}{\square} = \frac{15}{\square}$.

$\mathit{Remark}$ $\square$roceeding as in Example $\square$4 one may prove that the two positive numbers,

whose sum is $k$ and the sum of whose squares is minimum, are $\frac{k}{\square}$ and $\frac{k}{\square}$.

Example 35 Find the shortest distance of the point $(0, c)$ from the parabola $y = x^{\square}$,
where $0 \le c \le 5$.

Solution $\square$et $(h, k)$ be any point on the parabola $y = x^{\square}$. $\square$et $\square$ be the required distance
between $(h, k)$ and $(0, c)$. Then

$$\square = \sqrt{(h - 0)^{\square} + (k - c)^{\square}} = \sqrt{h^{\square} + (k - c)^{\square}} \qquad \dots (1)$$

Since $(h, k)$ lies on the parabola $y = x^{\square}$, we have $k = h^{\square}$. So (1) gives

$$\square \equiv \square(k) = \sqrt{k + (k - c)^{\square}}$$

or

$$\square'(k) = \frac{1 + \square(k - c)}{\square\sqrt{k + (k - c)^{\square}}}$$

$\square$ow

$\square'(k) = 0$ gives $k = \frac{\square c - 1}{\square}$

$\square$bserve that when $k < \frac{\square c - 1}{\square}$, then $\square(k - c) + 1 < 0$, i.e., $\square'(k) < 0$. Also when

$k > \frac{\square c - 1}{\square}$, then $\square'(k) > 0$. So, by first derivative test, $\square(k)$ is minimum at $k = \frac{\square c - 1}{\square}$.

Hence, the required shortest distance is given by

<!-- page 241 -->
$$\square \left( \frac{\boxminus c - 1}{\square} \right) = \sqrt{\frac{\boxminus c - 1}{\square} + \left( \frac{\boxminus c - 1}{\square} - c \right)^{\square}} = \frac{\sqrt{4c - 1}}{\square}$$

Note The reader may note that in Example $\square 5$, we have used first derivative
test instead of the second derivative test as the former is easy and short.

Example 36 $\square$et $A\square$and $\square\square$ be two vertical poles at
points $A$ and $\square$, respectively. If $A\square= 1\square$m, $\square\square = \square\square$m
and $A\square = \square 0$ m, then find the distance of a point $R$ on
$A\square$ from the point $A$ such that $R\square\square R\square\square$ is minimum.

Solution $\square$et R be a point on A $\square$ such that AR $= x$ m.
Then R $\square$ = ( $\square$ 0 $\square$x) m (as A $\square$ = $\square$ 0 m). From Fig $\square$ 1 $\square$
we have

$$R \Box^\square = A R^\square \Box A \Box^\square$$
and

$$R \Box^\square = R \Box^\square \Box \Box \Box^2$$
$$R \Box^\square \Box R \Box^\square = A R^\square \Box A \Box^\square \Box R \Box^\square \Box \Box \Box^\square$$
$$= x^\square \Box (1 \Box)^\square \Box (\Box 0 \Box x)^\square \Box (\Box \Box)^\square$$
$$= \Box x^\square \Box 40x \Box 1140$$

$\square$et                                     $\text{S} \equiv \text{S}(x) = \text{R} \square \square \text{R} \square \square = \square x \square 40x \square 1140.$
Therefore                                     $S'(x) = 4x \square 40.$

Therefore,
$\square$ow $S'(x) = 0$ gives $x = 10$. Also $S''(x) = 4$ $\square$ 0, for all $x$ and so $S''(10)$ $\square$ 0.
Therefore, by second derivative test, $x = 10$ is the point of local minima of S. Thus, the
distance of R from A on A $\square$ is AR = $x = 10$ m.

**Example 37** If length of three sides of a trapezium other than base are equal to $10\text{cm}$,

$$\square \square \square$$

C$\square$ on A$\square$. $\square$et A$\square$=$x$ cm. $\square$ote that $\Delta$A$\square$ $\square$ $\Delta\square\square$C. Therefore, $\square\square$=$x$ cm. Also, by
$\square$ythagoras theorem, $\square\square$=$ \square$C = $\sqrt{100 - x^2}$. $\square$et A be the area of the trape $\square$ium. Then

$$A \equiv A(x) = \frac{1}{2} \text{ (sum of parallel sides) (height)}$$

$$= \frac{1}{[} (\square x + 10 + 10) \left( \sqrt{100 - x^2} \right)$$

$$= (x+10) \left( \sqrt{100 - x^2} \right)$$

or
$$A'(x) = (x+10)\frac{(-\Box x)}{\Box \sqrt{100-x^\Box}} + \left(\sqrt{100-x^\Box}\right)$$
$$= \frac{-\Box^\Box - 10x + 100}{\sqrt{100-x^\Box}}$$

$\square$ow $\quad A'(x) = 0$ gives $\square x^0 \square 10x \square 100 = 0$, i.e., $x = 5$ and $x = \square 10$.

Since $x$ represents distance, it can not be negative.

So, $x = 5$. $\square$ow

$$A''(x) = \frac{\sqrt{100 - x^\square}(-4x - 10) - (-\square x^\square - 10x + 100)\frac{(-\square x)}{\square \sqrt{100 - x^\square}}}{100 - x^\square}$$

Thus, area of trapezium is maximum at $x=5$ and the area is given by

$$A(5) = (5+10)\sqrt{100 - (5)^2} = 15\sqrt{5} = 5\sqrt{cm}$$

Example 38 $\square$rove that the radius of the right circular cylinder of greatest curved

**Solution** $\square$et $\square$C $= r$ be the radius of the cone and $\square$A $= h$ be its height. $\square$et a cylinder
with radius $\square$E $= x$ inscribed in the given cone (Fig $\square$ $\square$0). The height $\square$E of the cylinder
is given by

$$\frac{\Box \text{E}}{\Box \text{A}} = \frac{\text{EC}}{\Box \text{C}} \quad (\text{since } \Delta \Box \text{EC} \quad \Box \Delta \text{A} \Box \text{C})$$

or $$\frac{\Box \mathrm{E}}{h} = \frac{r-x}{r}$$

or $\square \mathrm{E}=\frac{h(r-x)}{r}$

Fig 6.20

$\square$et S be the curved surface area of the given
cylinder. Then

$$S \equiv S(x) = \frac{\pi x h(r-x)}{r} = \frac{\pi h}{r} (rx - x^{\square})$$

or
$$\begin{cases} \mathrm{S}'(x) = \frac{\square \pi h}{r}(r - \square x) \\ \mathrm{S}''(x) = \frac{-4\pi h}{r} \end{cases}$$

$\square$ow $S'(x) = 0$ gives $x = \frac{r}{\square}$. Since $S''(x) \square 0$ for all $x$, $S''\left(\frac{r}{\square}\right) < 0$. So $x = \frac{r}{\square}$ is a

point of maxima of S. Hence, the radius of the cylinder of greatest curved surface area
which can be inscribed in a given cone is half of that of the cone.

6.6.1 Maximum and Minimum Values of a Function in a Closed Interval

$\square$et us consider a function $f$ given by

$$f(x) = x \ \square \ \square, x \in (0, 1)$$

$\square$bserve that the function is continuous on $(0, 1)$ and neither has a maximum value
nor has a minimum value. Further, we may note that the function even has neither a
local maximum value nor a local minimum value.

However, if we extend the domain of $f$ to the closed interval $\boxminus 0, 1 \boxminus$, then $f$ still may
not have a local maximum (minimum) values but it certainly does have maximum value
$\boxminus = f(1)$ and minimum value $\boxminus = f(0)$. The maximum value $\boxminus$ of $f$ at $x = 1$ is called
absolute maximum value (global maximum or greatest value) of $f$ on the interval
$\boxminus 0, 1 \boxminus$ Similarly, the minimum value $\boxminus$ of $f$ at $x = 0$ is called the absolute minimum
value (global minimum or least value) of $f$ on $\boxminus 0, 1 \boxminus$

Consider the graph given in Fig $\square\square$ of a continuous function defined on a closed
interval $\boxed{a}$, $d\boxed{}$ $\boxed{\text{bserve}}$ that the function $f$ has a local minima at $x = b$ and local

<!-- page 244 -->
Fig 6.21

minimum value is $f(b)$. The function also has a local maxima at $x=c$ and local maximum
value is $f(c)$.

Also from the graph, it is evident that $f$ has absolute maximum value $f(a)$ and
absolute minimum value $f(d)$. Further note that the absolute maximum (minimum)
value of $f$ is different from local maximum (minimum) value of $f$.

We will now state two results (without proof) regarding absolute maximum and
absolute minimum values of a function on a closed interval I.

**Theorem 5** $\square$et $f$ be a continuous function on an interval I = $\square a, b \square$ Then $f$ has the
absolute maximum value and $f$ attains it at least once in I. Also, $f$ has the absolute
minimum value and attains it at least once in I.

Theorem 6 $\square$et $f$ be a differentiable function on a closed interval I and let $c$ be any
interior point of I. Then

(i) $f'(c) = 0$ if $f$ attains its absolute maximum value at $c$.
(ii) $f'(c) = 0$ if $f$ attains its absolute minimum value at $c$.

In view of the above results, we have the following wor $\square$ing rule for finding absolute
maximum and $\bar{\text{or}}$ absolute minimum values of a function in a given closed interval
$\bar{a}, b \square$

Working Rule

Step 1 $\square$ Find all critical points of $f$ in the interval, i.e., find points $x$ where either
$f'(x) = 0$ or $f$ is not differentiable.

Step 2 $\square$ Ta $\square$ e the end points of the interval.

Step 3 $\square$ At all these points (listed in Step 1 and $\square$), calculate the values of $f$.

Step 4 $\square$ Identify the maximum and minimum values of $f$ out of the values calculated in
Step $\square$ This maximum value will be the absolute maximum (greatest) value of
$f$ and the minimum value will be the absolute minimum (least) value of $f$.

<!-- page 245 -->
$\square \square$ 0 MATHEMATICS

Example 39 Find the absolute maximum and minimum values of a function $f$ given by
$$f(x) = \square x^\square \square 15x^\square \square \square \square x \square 1 \text{ on the interval } \square 1, 5 \square$$

Solution We have
$$f(x) = \square x^\square \square 15x^\square \square \square \square x \square 1$$
or
$$f'(x) = \square x^\square \square 0x \square \square \square = \square (x \square \square) (x \square \square)$$
$\square$ote that $f'(x) = 0$ gives $x = \square$and $x = \square$
We shall now evaluate the value of $f$ at these points and at the end points of the
interval $\square 1, 5 \square$ i.e., at $x = 1, x = \square, x = \square$and at $x = 5$. So
$$f(1) = \square (1^\square) \square 15 (1^\square) \square \square \square (1) \square 1 = \square 4$$
$$f(\square) = \square (\square) \square 15 (\square) \square \square \square (\square) \square 1 = \square 9$$
$$f(\square) = \square (\square) \square 15 (\square) \square \square \square (\square) \square 1 = \square \square$$
$$f(5) = \square (5^\square) \square 15 (5^\square) \square \square \square (5) \square 1 = 5 \square$$
Thus, we conclude that absolute maximum value of $f$ on $\square 1, 5 \square$ is $5 \square$, occurring at
$x = 5$, and absolute minimum value of $f$ on $\square 1, 5 \square$ is $\square 4$ which occurs at $x = 1$.

Example 40 Find absolute maximum and minimum values of a function $f$ given by
$$f(x) = 1 \square \overline{x^\square} - \square x^\square, x \in \square + 1, 1 \square$$

Solution We have
$$f(x) = 1 \square \overline{x^\square} - \square x^\square$$
or
$$f'(x) = 1 \square \overline{x^\square} - \frac{\square}{x^\square} = \frac{\square (\square x - 1)}{x^\square}$$

Thus, $f'(x) = 0$ gives $x = \frac{1}{\square}$. Further note that $f'(x)$ is not defined at $x = 0$. So the
critical points are $x = 0$ and $x = \frac{1}{\square}$. $\square$ow evaluating the value of $f$ at critical points
$x = 0, \frac{1}{\square}$ and at end points of the interval $x = \square 1$ and $x = 1$, we have
$$f(\square 1) = 1 \square (-1)^\square - \square (-1)^\square = 1 \square$$
$$f(0) = 1 \square (0) \square \square (0) = 0$$

<!-- page 246 -->
A□□□ICATI□□ ☐F ☐ERI□ATI□ES

$$f\left(\frac{1}{\square}\right) = 1\left(\frac{1}{\square}\right)^{\frac{4}{\square}} - \square\left(\frac{1}{\square}\right)^{\frac{1}{\square}} = \frac{-9}{4}$$

$\square \square 1$

$$f(1) = 1 \Box (1)^{\frac{4}{\Box}} - \Box (1)^{\frac{1}{\Box}} = \Box$$

Hence, we conclude that absolute maximum value of $f$ is $1$ $\square$ that occurs at $x = \square 1$

and absolute minimum value of $f$ is $\frac{-9}{4}$ that occurs at $x = \frac{1}{\square}$.

Example 41 An Apache helicopter of enemy is flying along the curve given by
$y = x^{\square} \square \square$ A soldier, placed at $(\square, \square)$, wants to shoot down the helicopter when it is
nearest to him. Find the nearest distance.

**Solution** For each value of $x$, the helicopter $\bar{\text{s}}$ position is at point $(x, x^{\square} \ \square \ \square)$.
Therefore, the distance between the helicopter and the soldier placed at $(\square, \square)$ is

$$\sqrt{(x-\square)^{\square}+(x^{\square}+\square-\square)^{\square}} , \text{ i.e., } \sqrt{(x-\square)^{\square}+x^{4}} .$$

$\square$ et $f(x) = (x \square \square)^\square \square x^4$
or $f'(x) = \square (x \square \square) \square 4x^\square = \square (x \square 1) (\square x^\square \square \square x \square \square)$

Thus, $f'(x) = 0$ gives $x = 1$ or $x^0 \square \square x \square \square = 0$ for which there are no real roots.
Also, there are no end points of the interval to be added to the set for which $f'$ is zero,
i.e., there is only one point, namely, $x = 1$. The value of $f$ at this point is given by
$f(1) = (1 \square \square)^0 \square (1)^4 = 5$. Thus, the distance between the solder and the helicopter is
$$\sqrt{f(1)} = \sqrt{5} \text{ .}$$

$\square$ote that $\sqrt{5}$ is either a maximum value or a minimum value. Since

$$\sqrt{f(0)} = \sqrt{(0 - \square)^2 + (0)^4} = \square > \sqrt{5} ,$$

it follows that $\sqrt{5}$ is the minimum value of $\sqrt{f(x)}$. Hence, $\sqrt{5}$ is the minimum
distance between the soldier and the helicopter.

Miscellaneous Examples

Example 42 A car starts from a point $\square$ at time $t=0$ seconds and stops at point $\square$. The
distance $x$, in metres, covered by it, in $t$ seconds is given by

$$x = t^{\square} \left( \square - \frac{t}{\square} \right)$$

Find the time ta $\square$ en by it to reach $\square$ and also find distance between $\square$ and $\square$.

Solution $\square$ et $v$ be the velocity of the car at $t$ seconds.

$\square$ ow $x = t^{\square} \left( \square - \frac{t}{\square} \right)$

Therefore $v = \frac{dx}{dt} = 4t \square t^{\square} = t(4 \square t)$

Thus, $v=0$ gives $t=0$ and $\square$ or $t=4$.

$\square$ ow $v=0$ at $\square$ as well as at $\square$ and at $\square$, $t=0$. So, at $\square$, $t=4$. Thus, the car will
reach the point $\square$ after 4 seconds. Also the distance travelled in 4 seconds is given by

$$x_{\frac{\square}{t=4}} = 4^{\square} \left( \square - \frac{4}{\square} \right) = 1 \square \left( \frac{\square}{\square} \right) = \frac{\square}{\square} \text{ m}$$

<!-- page 250 -->
Example 43 A water $\tan \square$ has the shape of an inverted right circular cone with its axis
vertical and vertex lowermost. Its semi $\square$ vertical angle is $\tan ^\top$ (0.5). Water is poured
into it at a constant rate of 5 cubic metre per hour. Find the rate at which the level of
the water is rising at the instant when the depth of water in the $\tan \square$ is 4 m.

Solution $\square$et $r$, $h$ and $\alpha$ be as in Fig $\square\square\square$ Then $\tan \alpha = \frac{r}{h}$.

So $\alpha = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{r}{h}\right).$

$\square$ut $\qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \alpha = \tan^{-1}(0.5) \quad \text{(given)}$

Fig 6.22

or $\frac{r}{h} = 0.5$

or $r = \frac{h}{\Gamma}$

$\square$et $\square$ be the volume of the cone. Then

$$\square = \frac{1}{\square} \pi r^\square h = \frac{1}{\square} \pi \left( \frac{h}{\square} \right)^\square h = \frac{\pi h^\square}{1 \square}$$

Therefore $\frac{d\square}{dt} = \frac{d}{dh}\left(\frac{\pi h^{\square}}{1\square}\right)\cdot\frac{dh}{dt}$ (by Chain Rule)

$$= \frac{\pi}{4} h^{\square} \frac{dh}{dt}$$

$\square$ow rate of change of volume, i.e., $\frac{d \square}{dt} = 5 \text{ m}^{-1}\text{h}$ and $h = 4 \text{ m}$.

Therefore $5 = \frac{\pi}{4}(4)^{\square} \cdot \frac{dh}{dt}$

or $$\frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{5}{4\pi} = \frac{\square 5}{\square} \text{mh} \left( \pi = \frac{\square}{\square} \right)$$

Thus, the rate of change of water level is $\frac{5}{\text{I}} \text{ m}\hbar$.

Example 44 A man of height $\square$metres wal$\square$s at a uniform speed of 5 $\text{m}$h away from
a lamp post which is $\square$metres high. Find the rate at which the length of his shadow
increases.

<!-- page 251 -->
**Solution** In Fig $\square\square$, $\square$et $\text{A} \square$ be the lamp $\square$post, the
lamp being at the position $\square$ and let $\text{M} \square$ be the man
at a particular time $t$ and let $\text{AM} = l$ metres. Then,
$\text{MS}$ is the shadow of the man. $\square$et $\text{MS} = s$ metres.

Fig 6.23

$\square$ote that                                                                    $\Delta\text{MS} \square \quad \square \Delta\text{AS} \square$

or $$\frac{\text{MS}}{\text{AS}} = \frac{\text{M}\square}{\text{A}\square}$$

or $AS = \Box s$ (as $M \Box = \Box$and $A \Box = \Box$(given))

Thus                                                               AM = $\Box s$ $\Box s$ = $\Box s$. $\Box$ ut AM = $l$
So                                                                    $l$ = $\Box s$

Therefore $\frac{dl}{dt} = \left[ \frac{ds}{dt} \right]$

Since $\frac{dl}{dt} = 5 \text{ m.h.}$ Hence, the length of the shadow increases at the rate $\frac{5}{\text{m.h.}}$

Example 45 Find the equation of the normal to the curve $x^{\square} = 4y$ which passes through
the point $(1, \square)$.

Solution $\square$ifferentiating $x^{\square} = 4y$ with respect to $x$, we get

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{x}{\square}$$

$\square$et $(h, k)$ be the coordinates of the point of contact of the normal to the curve
$x^{\square} = 4y$. $\square$ow, slope of the tangent at $(h, k)$ is given by

$$\left. \frac{dy}{dx} \right]_{(h, k)} = \frac{h}{\square}$$

Hence, slope of the normal at $(h, k) = \frac{-[}{h}$

Therefore, the equation of normal at $(h, k)$ is

$$y \ \square \ k = \frac{-\square}{h}(x - h) \hspace{100pt} ... (1)$$

Since it passes through the point $(1, \square)$, we have

$$\square - k = \frac{-\square}{h}(1-h) \quad \text{or} \quad k = \square + \frac{\square}{h}(1-h) \qquad \dots (\square)$$

<!-- page 252 -->
A□□□ICATI□□ ☐F ☐ERI□ATI□ES

$$\square \square \square$$

Since $(h, k)$ lies on the curve $x^{\square} = 4y$, we have

$$h^{\square} = 4k \hspace{15em} \dots (\square)$$

From ($\square$) and ($\square$), we have $h = \square$and $k = 1$. Substituting the values of $h$ and $k$ in (1),
we get the required equation of normal as

$$y-1 = \frac{-\square}{[} (x-\square) \text{ or } x \ \square y = \square$$

Example 46 Find the equation of tangents to the curve

Example 46 Find the equation of tangents to the curve
$$y = \cos(x \square y), \square \square \pi \le x \le \square \pi$$
that are parallel to the line $x \square \square y = 0$.

Solution $\square$ differentiating $y = \cos(x \square y)$ with respect to $x$, we have
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{-\sin(x + y)}{1 + \sin(x + y)}$$
or slope of tangent at $(x, y) = \frac{-\sin(x + y)}{1 + \sin(x + y)}$$Since the tangents to the given curve are parallel to the line $x \square \square y = 0$, whose slope \\ is $\frac{-1}{\square}$, we have$$\frac{-\sin(x + y)}{1 + \sin(x + y)} = \frac{-1}{\square}$$
or $\sin(x \square y) = 1$
or $x \square y = n\pi \square (\square 1)^n \frac{\pi}{\square}, \ n \in \mathbf{Z}$
Then $y = \cos(x \square y) = \cos\left(n\pi + (-1)^n \frac{\pi}{\square}\right), \ n \in \mathbf{Z}$
$= 0$, for all $n \in \mathbf{Z}$
Also, since $-\square \pi \le x \le \square \pi$, we get $x = \frac{-\square \pi}{\square}$ and $x = \frac{\pi}{\square}$. Thus, tangents to the
given curve are parallel to the line $x \square \square y = 0$ only at points $\left(\frac{-\square \pi}{\square}, 0\right)$ and $\left(\frac{\pi}{\square}, 0\right)$.
Therefore, the required equation of tangents are

<!-- page 253 -->
$$y \square 0 = \frac{-1}{\square} \left( x + \frac{\square \pi}{\square} \right) \quad \text{or} \quad \square x + 4y + \square \pi = 0$$

and $y \square 0 = \frac{-1}{\square} \left( x - \frac{\pi}{\square} \right) \quad \text{or} \quad \square x + 4y - \pi = 0$

Example 47 Find intervals in which the function given by

$$f(x) = \frac{\square}{10}x^4 - \frac{4}{5}x^{\square} - \square x^{\square} + \frac{\square}{\square}x + 11$$

is (a) strictly increasing (b) strictly decreasing.

Solution We have

$$f(x) = \frac{\square}{10} x^4 - \frac{4}{5} x^{\square} - \square x^{\square} + \frac{\square}{5} x + 11$$

Example 48 Show that the function $f$ given by
$$f(x) = \tan^{-1}(\sin x \square \cos x), x \square 0$$
is always an strictly increasing function in $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{4}\right)$.

Solution We have
$$f(x) = \tan^{-1}(\sin x \square \cos x), x \square 0$$
Therefore
$$f'(x) = \frac{1}{1 + (\sin x + \cos x)^2} (\cos x - \sin x)$$
$$= \frac{\cos x - \sin x}{\square + \sin \square x} \qquad \text{(on simplification)}$$

$\square$ote that $\square \square \sin \square x \square 0$ for all $x$ in $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{4}\right)$.

Therefore $f'(x) \square 0$ if $\cos x \square \sin x \square 0$
or $f'(x) \square 0$ if $\cos x \square \sin x$ or $\cot x \square 1$

$\square$ow $\cot x \square 1$ if $\tan x \square 1$, i.e., if $0 < x < \frac{\pi}{4}$

Thus $f'(x) \square 0$ in $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{4}\right)$

Hence $f$ is strictly increasing function in $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{4}\right)$.

**Example 49** A circular disc of radius $\square$ cm is being heated. $\square$ue to expansion, its
radius increases at the rate of $0.05 \text{ cm}\bar{\text{s}}$. Find the rate at which its area is increasing
when radius is $\square\square\text{cm}$.

<!-- page 255 -->
Solution $\square$et $r$ be the radius of the given disc and A be its area. Then

$$\mathbf{A} = \pi r^{\square}$$

or $$\frac{dA}{dt} = \pi r \frac{dr}{dt} \qquad \text{(by Chain Rule)}$$

$\square$ow approximate rate of increase of radius $= dr = \frac{dr}{dt} \Delta t = 0.05 \text{ cm}$ $\bar{\text{s}}$.

Therefore, the approximate rate of increase in area is given by

$$dA = \frac{dA}{dt}(\Delta t) = \square \pi r \left( \frac{dr}{dt} \Delta t \right)$$
$$= \square \pi (\square \square) (0.05) = 0. \square \square 0 \pi \text{ cm} \square \bar{\text{s}} \quad (r = \square \square \text{cm})$$

Example 51 Manufacturer can sell $x$ items at a price of rupees $\left(5 - \frac{x}{100}\right)$ each. The


cost price of $x$ items is Rs $\left(\frac{x}{5} + 500\right)$. Find the number of items he should sell to earn


maximum profit.


Solution $\square$et $S(x)$ be the selling price of $x$ items and let $C(x)$ be the cost price of $x$
items. Then, we have


$$S(x) = \left(5 - \frac{x}{100}\right)x = 5x - \frac{x^{\square}}{100}$$


and


$$C(x) = \frac{x}{5} + 500$$


Thus, the profit function $\square(x)$ is given by


$$\square(x) = S(x) - C(x) = 5x - \frac{x^{\square}}{100} - \frac{x}{5} - 500$$


i.e.


$$\square(x) = \frac{\square 4}{5}x - \frac{x^{\square}}{100} - 500$$


or


$$\square'(x) = \frac{\square 4}{5} - \frac{x}{50}$$


$\square$ow $\square'(x) = 0$ gives $x = \square 40$. Also $\square''(x) = \frac{-1}{50}$. So $\square''((\square 40) = \frac{-1}{50} < 0$


Thus, $x = \square 40$ is a point of maxima. Hence, the manufacturer can earn maximum
profit, if he sells $\square 40$ items.

Summary

$\blacklozenge$ If a quantity $y$ varies with another quantity $x$, satisfying some rule $y = f(x)$,
then $\frac{dy}{dx}$ (or $f'(x)$ ) represents the rate of change of $y$ with respect to $x$ and
$$\left. \frac{dy}{dx} \right|_{x=x_0} \text{ (or } f'(x_0) \text{ ) represents the rate of change of } y \text{ with respect to } x \text{ at}$$
$$x = x_0 \text{ .}$$
$\blacklozenge$ If two variables $x$ and $y$ are varying with respect to another variable $t$, i.e., if
$x = f(t)$ and $y = g(t)$ , then by Chain Rule
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy}{dt} \Big/ \frac{dx}{dt} \text{ , if } \frac{dx}{dt} \neq 0 \text{ .}$$
$\blacklozenge$ A function $f$ is said to be
(a) increasing on an interval $(a, b)$ if
$x_1 \square x_0$ in $(a, b) \Rightarrow f(x_1) \leq f(x_0)$ for all $x_1, x_0 \in (a, b)$.

<!-- page 260 -->
A□□□ICATI□□ ☐F ☐ERI□ATI□ES

$\square 45$

Alternatively, if $f'(x) \geq 0$ for each $x$ in $(a, b)$
(b) decreasing on $(a, b)$ if
$$x_1 \square x_{\square} \text{ in } (a, b) \Rightarrow f(x_1) \geq f(x_{\square}) \text{ for all } x_1, x_{\square} \in (a, b).$$
Alternatively, if $f'(x) \leq 0$ for each $x$ in $(a, b)$
$\diamond$ The equation of the tangent at $(x_0, y_0)$ to the curve $y = f(x)$ is given by
$$y - y_0 = \left. \frac{dy}{dx} \right]_{(x_0, y_0)} (x - x_0)$$
$\diamond$ If $\frac{dy}{dx}$ does not exist at the point $(x_0, y_0)$, then the tangent at this point is
parallel to the y axis and its equation is $x = x_0$.
$\diamond$ If tangent to a curve $y = f(x)$ at $x = x_0$ is parallel to $x$ axis, then $\left. \frac{dy}{dx} \right]_{x=x_0} = 0$.
$\diamond$ Equation of the normal to the curve $y = f(x)$ at a point $(x_0, y_0)$ is given by
$$y - y_0 = \frac{-1}{\left. \frac{dy}{dx} \right]_{(x_0, y_0)}} (x - x_0)$$
$\diamond$ If $\frac{dy}{dx}$ at the point $(x_0, y_0)$ is zero, then equation of the normal is $x = x_0$.
$\diamond$ If $\frac{dy}{dx}$ at the point $(x_0, y_0)$ does not exist, then the normal is parallel to $x$ axis
and its equation is $y = y_0$.
$\diamond$ $\square$ et $y = f(x)$, $\Delta x$ be a small increment in $x$ and $\Delta y$ be the increment in $y$
corresponding to the increment in $x$, i.e., $\Delta y = f(x \square \Delta x) \square f(x)$. Then $dy$
given by
$$dy = f'(x)dx \text{ or } dy = \left( \frac{dy}{dx} \right) \Delta x.$$
is a good approximation of $\Delta y$ when $dx = \Delta x$ is relatively small and we denote
it by $dy \approx \Delta y$.
$\diamond$ A point $c$ in the domain of a function $f$ at which either $f'(c) = 0$ or $f$ is not
differentiable is called a critical point of $f$.

<!-- page 261 -->
$\blacklozenge$ \textbf{\textit{First Derivative Test}} $\Box$et $f$ be a function defined on an open interval I. $\Box$et
$f$ be continuous at a critical point $c$ in I. Then
(i) If $f'(x)$ changes sign from positive to negative as $x$ increases through $c$,
i.e., if $f'(x) \Box 0$ at every point sufficiently close to and to the left of $c$,
and $f'(x) \Box 0$ at every point sufficiently close to and to the right of $c$,
then $c$ is a point of \textit{local maxima}.
(ii) If $f'(x)$ changes sign from negative to positive as $x$ increases through $c$,
i.e., if $f'(x) \Box 0$ at every point sufficiently close to and to the left of $c$,
and $f'(x) \Box 0$ at every point sufficiently close to and to the right of $c$,
then $c$ is a point of \textit{local minima}.
(iii) If $f'(x)$ does not change sign as $x$ increases through $c$, then $c$ is neither
a point of local maxima nor a point of local minima. Infact, such a point
is called \textit{point of inflexion}.
$\blacklozenge$ \textbf{\textit{Second Derivative Test}} $\Box$et $f$ be a function defined on an interval I and
$c \in \text{I}$. $\Box$et $f$ be twice differentiable at $c$. Then
(i) $x = c$ is a point of local maxima if $f'(c) = 0$ and $f''(c) \Box 0$
The values $f(c)$ is local maximum value of $f$.
(ii) $x = c$ is a point of local minima if $f'(c) = 0$ and $f''(c) \Box 0$
In this case, $f(c)$ is local minimum value of $f$.
(iii) The test fails if $f'(c) = 0$ and $f''(c) = 0$.
In this case, we go bac $\Box$to the first derivative test and find whether $c$ is
a point of maxima, minima or a point of inflexion.
$\blacklozenge$ Wor $\Box$ing rule for finding absolute maxima and $\Box$or absolute minima
\textbf{Step 1:} Find all critical points of $f$ in the interval, i.e., find points $x$ where
either $f'(x) = 0$ or $f$ is not differentiable.
\textbf{Step 2:} Ta $\Box$e the end points of the interval.
\textbf{Step 3:} At all these points (listed in Step 1 and $\Box$), calculate the values of $f$.
\textbf{Step 4:} Identify the maximum and minimum values of $f$ out of the values
calculated in Step $\Box$ This maximum value will be the absolute maximum
value of $f$ and the minimum value will be the absolute minimum value of $f$.
